Quantitative homogenization of convex Hamilton–Jacobi equations in The Wasserstein space Thanks: I. Ekren is partially supported by the NSF grant DMS-2406240.
Zhiyan Ding, Ibrahim Ekren, Yuxi Han, Antonios Zitridis

Abstract
. We study a homogenization problem for first-order Hamilton–Jacobi equations in the Wasserstein space with a convex Hamiltonian. We show that the solution U ε U^{\varepsilon} , which is the value function of a mean field control problem, converges uniformly as ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 to the solution of a limiting Hamilton–Jacobi equation whose Hamiltonian is obtained through a suitable cell problem. Furthermore, we establish quantitative rates of convergence. Under general assumptions with multiscale dependence, we prove that the rate of convergence is O ⁡ ( ε ) O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}) . When the Hamiltonian depends only on the fast variable and the momentum, we establish the sharp convergence rate O ⁡ ( ε ) O(\varepsilon) . To the best of our knowledge, this is the first quantitative convergence result extending the optimal rate for first-order Hamilton–Jacobi equations in finite dimensions to the Wasserstein space. Finally, we show that our analysis extends to dynamic optimal transport problems, where the terminal condition imposes a constraint on the final distribution.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation and setup of the problem

Let
\varepsilon,T>0
and
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. This paper is devoted to studying the behavior as
\varepsilon\to 0
of the solution
of the first-order Hamilton–Jacobi (HJ) equation in the Wasserstein space of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moment (denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
):

(
\mathrm{HJB}_{\varepsilon}
)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}U^{\varepsilon}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}H(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},x,D_{m}U^{\varepsilon}(t,m,x),m)m(dx)=0,&(t,m)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
U^{\varepsilon}(T,m)=G(m),&m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{cases}

where
H\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is a given Hamiltonian and
G\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is the terminal constraint. Here
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional torus and the derivative

D_{m}U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of the function
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is the Wasserstein derivative
1
1

1

We refer to
[
CD+18
, Section 5]
.
.

Hamilton–Jacobi equations posed on spaces of probability measures have attracted considerable attention in recent years. Such equations arise in several areas including mean field games, mean field control, optimal transport and filtering. For a sample of recent works, we refer to
[
CDLL19
]
for mean field games and mean field control,
[
Ber24
]
for optimal transport,
[
Mar23
,
BEHZ25
]
for filtering.
In particular, equations of the form (

HJB
ε

) (especially when the Hamiltonian
H
does not have the
x/\varepsilon
argument) arise as dynamic programming equations associated with mean field control problems. These problems study the collective behavior of a large population of interacting agents/particles who cooperate to minimize a common cost functional that might depend on the distribution of the population; see
[
CD+18
,
CDLL19
]
for surveys on the topic. More specifically in our setup, assuming that
p\mapsto H(x/\varepsilon,x,p,m)
is convex, then under mild assumptions on
H,\;G
, (

HJB
ε

)
is the dynamic programming equation
[
DPT22b
]
satisfied by the value function
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
of the mean field control problem

(1.1)

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(X_{s}\right)\right)ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right\},

where we have considered a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
with no atoms that supports an
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion
B
,
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
is the law of the state process
X
at time
s\in[t,T]
, and the random variable
X_{t}
is assumed to be independent of the Brownian motion
B
and such that
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The control set
\mathcal{A}
is

(1.2)

\mathcal{A}:=\left\{\alpha\colon[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\;\alpha\text{ is }\mathbb{F}\text{-progressively measurable and }\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\alpha_{s}|^{2}ds\right]<\infty\right\},

the dynamics of the state process
X
are determined from the control
\alpha
via
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds
, and
L:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is the Legendre transform of the Hamiltonian
H
:

(1.3)

L(y,x,v,m):=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{-p\cdot v-H(y,x,p,m)\big\}.

The dependence of
H
and
L
on the fast variable
x/\varepsilon
models the situation where the agents in the mean field control problem (
1.1
) evolve in an environment that exhibits rapid spatial variations.
The parameter
\varepsilon>0
is the scale of these variations. From an application point of view, this problem models a system of interacting agents/particles moving in a heterogeneous landscape, or having fluctuating cost.

Our first goal in this paper is to establish the asymptotic behavior of the value function
U^{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
1.1
), as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Intuitively, when the scale of the heterogeneities becomes very small (
\varepsilon\to 0
), the oscillations of the Hamiltonian average out, and one expects
U^{\varepsilon}
to converge to a function
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
that can be described as the solution of a limiting HJ equation on the Wasserstein space with an effective Hamiltonian, in analogy with classical homogenization results for HJ equations in finite dimensions (see
[
LPV87
]
and subsequent works, for instance,
[
CDI01
,
MTY19
,
TY21
]
).
In this work, we rigorously prove this convergence under general assumptions on the Hamiltonian
H
and the terminal cost
G
. Furthermore, we establish a quantitative convergence rate for general multiscale settings and obtain the optimal rate in the non-multiscale setting. To our knowledge, this is the first quantitative result for HJ equations in the Wasserstein space
and it extends previously known results for HJ equations in finite dimensions; we refer to
[
Tra21
]
for an overview of results in finite dimensions.

As is the case in finite dimensions, HJ equations in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
do not necessarily have classical solutions (we present an example in Remark
3.9
(i) for (

HJB
ε

)), therefore a large number of recent works have been devoted to developing an appropriate notion of viscosity sub/super-solution and to establishing a comparison principle. We refer to
[
CKT23
,
DS24
,
BCE+25
,
BEZ25
,
BEHZ25
,
CTQ25
,
DJS25b
,
ZTZ24
,
SY24
,
MSY23
,
Ber24
]
, among others. Unlike in finite dimensions
[
CIL92
]
, there are several reasonable definitions of viscosity solutions for equations posed in the Wasserstein space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Most relevant to the PDE (

HJB
ε

) that we are interested in are the works
[
GT19
,
GMS21
]
, which consider viscosity solutions in the “lifted” sense of P.L. Lions.
In this paper, we adopt this notion of viscosity solutions and we refer to them as
L
-viscosity solutions, or simply as viscosity solutions if there is no confusion. We defer their precise definition to Section
3
Definition
3.7
.

The second goal of this paper is to show that the above homogenization result for (

HJB
ε

) implies a homogenization result for dynamic optimal transport or mean field planning problems. In this case, the terminal condition
G
is allowed to take the value
+\infty
and imposes a constraint on the final distribution of agents. In particular, suppose that
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
G(m)=+\infty
if
m\neq\nu
and
0
otherwise, in (
1.1
). Then,
U^{\varepsilon}
becomes a dynamic optimal transport problem
U_{ot}^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
with

(1.4)

U_{ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\;:=\;\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}}\;\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}L\Big(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\Big)\,ds\Bigg],

where the associated state process
X=(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
is such that
X_{t}
is independent of the Brownian motion
B
with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu
, its dynamics are determined by the control
\alpha
through
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}\,ds,
and the infimum is taken over all controls in the set

(1.5)

\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}:=\left\{\alpha\colon[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\;\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\;\exists X_{t},\;\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu,\;\mathcal{L}(X_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\alpha_{s}ds)=\nu\right\}.

There is extensive literature in the finite dimensional setting on the qualitative behavior (
\Gamma
-limit theory) as
\varepsilon\to 0
of functionals of the form

(1.6)

\int_{t}^{T}l(\gamma(s)/\varepsilon,\dot{\gamma}(s))ds,

where
l\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma\colon[t,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an absolutely continuous curve with derivative
\dot{\gamma}
(for example,
[
Wei91
]
and references therein); we refer to the books
[
BD98
,
JKO12
]
and the references therein for a detailed account of homogenization of integral functionals. In this paper, our second goal is to prove that
U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}
from (
1.4
) has a limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
and derive a rate of convergence. Besides being quantitative, the presence of the measure argument
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
in (
1.4
) yields that our result also provides an extension of previously established qualitative results for finite dimensional problems such as (
1.6
) to infinite dimensions (for instance, see
[
CDI01
,
TY21
]
). A similar qualitative result in infinite dimensions was obtained in the work of Gangbo and Tudorascu
[
GT12
]
, which considers curves
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
in
L^{p},\;p\geq 1
.

In the remainder of this introduction, we first review the existing results for HJ equations in finite dimensions and then state precisely our main results. We then turn to a comparison of our results with the existing literature on homogenization of HJ equations in the Wasserstein space, and give an overview of our proof techniques.

1.2.
Description and statement of main results

Our main results depend on two different sets of assumptions for the Hamiltonian
H=H(y,x,p,m)\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
.
The first one considers the multiscale homogenization problem, where
H
is a general function satisfying the conditions in Assumption (A1)
(section
2
). The second assumes that
H(y,x,p,m)
, where
y\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, does not depend on the
x
and
m
variables (Assumption (A1’)) (no multiscale effect). In the latter case, when there is no confusion, we write
H(y,p)
instead of
H(y,x,p,m)
.

To better explain our results and where they place in the literature, we
first focus on the simpler case where
H=H(y,p),\;(y,p)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, does not depend on
(x,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and we describe the analogous finite-dimensional homogenization problem. Its dynamic programming equation is the classical first-order HJ equation

(1.7)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+H\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},D_{x}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\right)=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u^{\varepsilon}(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

where
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous terminal cost. The function

u^{\varepsilon}
admits the variational representation

u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\;=\;\inf_{\alpha}\,\left\{\int_{t}^{T}L\!\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},\alpha_{s}\right)ds+g(X_{T})\right\},

with state dynamics
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}\,ds
,
X_{t}=x
, and
L
the Legendre
transform of
H
in the momentum variable. This is the deterministic,
single-particle analogue of the mean-field control problem
(
1.1
): a single agent in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
replaces a population
distributed according to
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and the terminal cost

g
replaces the measure-dependent terminal cost
G
.

Under coercivity and continuity assumptions on
H
and continuity of
g
,
Lions–Papanicolaou–Varadhan
[
LPV87
]
showed that

u^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}u
locally uniformly, where
u\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is the viscosity solution to the effective problem

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}u(t,x)+\overline{H}(D_{x}u(t,x))=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases}

Here,
\overline{H}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is called the effective Hamiltonian;

\overline{H}(p)
is defined as the unique constant such that the cell problem

(1.8)

H(y,p+D_{y}v(y;p))=\overline{H}(p),\;\;y\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

admits a continuous viscosity solution
v=v(\cdot;p)\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
called a corrector. We refer to
[
Tra21
, Section 3]

or Proposition
2.2
for the properties of the effective Hamiltonian
inherited from
H
.

From the definition of
\overline{H}
, we can heuristically write the expansion

(1.9)

u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\approx u(t,x)+\varepsilon v(x/\varepsilon;D_{x}u(t,x)),

which suggests a rate of convergence for
u^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}u
of
O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore, following the qualitative homogenization result of Lions–Papanicolaou–Varadhan, the question of quantitative homogenization for (
1.7
) was addressed. Under the assumption that
g
is Lipschitz and
H
is coercive and locally Lipschitz, Capuzzo–Dolceta–Ishii
[
CDI01
]
obtained a rate of
O(\varepsilon^{1/3})
using the perturbed test function method. Under the additional assumption that
H
is convex in
p
, Mitake–Tran–Yu
[
MTY19
]
proved a rate of
O(\varepsilon)
in dimensions
d=2
and a rate of
O(\varepsilon^{1/2})
in dimensions
d\geq 3
using weak KAM theory. Finally, Tran–Yu
[
TY21
]
obtained the optimal rate
O(\varepsilon)
in the setting of
[
MTY19
]
for any finite dimension
d
, which completes the study in the framework of convex Hamiltonians depending only on the fast variable and the momentum. For further developments and extensions in various directions, we refer to
[
HJ23
,
HJMT25
,
MN26
,
HT25
,
MS23
,
MN25
,
HTZ25
,
MNT25
]
. For earlier nearly optimal convergence rates in the convex setting, we refer to
[
Tu20
,
Coo22
]
and the references therein.

The assumptions on
H
and
g
in
[
MTY19
,
TY21
]
help by giving additional structure to the solution
u^{\varepsilon}
of (
1.7
) in the form of the control formulation:

(1.10)

u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\inf_{\gamma\in AC([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;\gamma(t)=x}\left\{\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(s)\right)ds+g(\gamma(T))\right\},\;\;(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
AC([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all absolutely continuous curves in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\gamma(t)=x
is the initial position and
\dot{\gamma}(s)
is the speed of
\gamma
. A control formulation can be written for the solution of equation (

HJB
ε

). More specifically, our assumptions from Section
2
((A1’)–(A2) and (A1)–(A2)) guarantee that (
1.1
) is the unique viscosity solution of (

HJB
ε

)
2
2

2

See
[
GMS21
, Theorem 7.4]
for the uniqueness and
[
DPT22b
]
for the dynamic programming principle that leads to the viscosity solution property.
.

The above heuristic connection
3
3

3

A more precise connection between (

HJB
ε

) and HJ equations in finite dimensions will be given in section
3
.
between our problem and homogenization of HJ equations in finite dimensions suggests that an analogous expansion to (
1.9
) can be expected for the solution
U^{\varepsilon}
to (

HJB
ε

). Our first result is that
U^{\varepsilon}
converges with a rate of
O(\varepsilon)
to a solution of

(
\mathrm{HJB1}
)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}U+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{H}(D_{m}U(t,m,x))m(dx)=0,&(t,m)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
U(T,m)=G(m),&m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{cases}

where
\overline{H}
is the analog of (
1.8
) in the Wasserstein space. In particular, we have the following theorem, which extends the above works to the infinite dimensional setting
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
H,G
satisfy assumptions (A1’) and (A2) in Section
2
. Then,
p\mapsto\overline{H}(p)
is convex and (

HJB1

) has a unique viscosity solution in the sense of Definition
3.7
. In addition, for each
\varepsilon>0
, let
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the viscosity solution to (

HJB
ε

), and let
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the viscosity solution to (

HJB1

). Then,
U^{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
U
on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and there exists a constant
C=C\big(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G}\big)>0

such that

(1.11)

\bigl|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\bigr|\leq C\,\varepsilon,\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],\;m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Here,
C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G}
are the constants that appear in assumptions (A1’) and (A2).

The above theorem extends the previously known qualitative/quantitative homogenization results for the finite dimensional control problem
u^{\varepsilon}
in (
1.10
) to infinite dimensions and achieve the optimal convergence rate. In particular, a random initial position (
X_{t}
is random) and a random speed
\alpha
are allowed, as well as a terminal cost
G
that depends on the law of
X_{T}
and not necessarily its value. The proof is developed in Theorem
5.2
for the rate of convergence, in section
5.2
for the characterization of the limit, and in Proposition
5.5
for the example verifying the optimality of the rate.

Our second result extends Theorem
1.1
to the more general setting of assumptions (A1)–(A2) from Section
2
, where the Hamiltonian may depend on both the macroscopic variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the probability measure
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In this case, for given
(x,p,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the cell problem takes the form

(1.12)

H(y,x,p+D_{y}v(y;x,p,m),m)=\overline{H}(x,p,m),\;\;y\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
\overline{H}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is the effective Hamiltonian
4
4

4

We still denote the effective Hamiltonian by
\overline{H}
, because if
H
in (
1.12
) is independent of
x,m
, then
\overline{H}(x,p,m)
is the effective Hamiltonian constructed in (
1.8
).
. We expect that
U^{\varepsilon}
in this case will converge to a viscosity solution of

(
\mathrm{HJB2}
)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}U+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{H}(x,D_{m}U(t,m,x),m)m(dx)=0,&(t,m)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
U(T,m)=G(m),&m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{cases}

and we further establish an
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
convergence rate. The following is proved in Section
6
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
H,G
satisfy assumptions (A1) and (A2). Then, (

HJB2

) has a unique viscosity solution. For each
\varepsilon>0
, let
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the viscosity solution to (

HJB
ε

), and let
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the viscosity solution to (

HJB2

). Then,
U^{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
U
on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C=C\left(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},C_{G},K_{0}\right)>0
such that for any
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq\begin{cases}C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},&T-t\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\[4.0pt]
C\min\{T-t,\varepsilon\},&0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

The obtained rate of convergence is closely related to the multiscale result in finite-dimensional spaces established in
[
HJ23
, Theorem 1.1]
, where the rate is shown to be optimal, at least for
T-t\in[0,\varepsilon]
. Although we are unable to establish optimality in the present setting, our result may be viewed as an extension of the finite-dimensional convergence rate to the infinite-dimensional setting.

It is worth noting that under Assumption (A1) (resp. (A1’)) on
H
,
\overline{H}
satisfies certain convexity, coercivity and local Lipschitz assumptions (Proposition
2.2
) which are enough to characterize the unique viscosity solution
U
of (

HJB2

) (resp. (

HJB1

)) in terms of the optimal control problem

(1.13)

\displaystyle U(t,m)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\overline{L}(X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left(\text{resp. }U(t,m)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\overline{L}(\alpha_{s})ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right\}\right).

Here,
\mathcal{A}
is given in (
1.2
) and
\overline{L}=\overline{L}(x,v,m)\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\overline{L}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
) is the Legendre transform with respect to the
p
variable of the effective Hamiltonian
\overline{H}(x,p,m)
(resp.
\overline{H}(p)
)

(1.14)

\overline{L}(x,v,m):=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{-p\cdot v-\overline{H}(x,p,m)\big\}\;\;\big(\text{resp. }\overline{L}(v):=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{-p\cdot v-\overline{H}(p)\big\}\big).

Finally, going beyond the case where
G(m)<\infty
in (
1.1
), we obtain a quantitative homogenization result for the dynamic optimal transport problem (
1.4
). By using similar arguments as in the proofs of Theorem
1.1
or Theorem
1.2
– depending on whether
H
satisfies (A1) or (A1’)
– we have for
t\in[0,T]
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu),

where

(1.15)

U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\overline{L}(X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))ds\right]\right\}

is the dynamic optimal transport problem with cost
\overline{L}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that assumptions (A1)–(A2) are in place. For
\varepsilon>0
consider the function
U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
defined in (
1.4
) and fix
(t,\mu,\nu)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. There exists a constant
C
depending on
d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},C_{G}
and
K_{0}
such that the following inequality holds

|U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)-U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)|\leq C\left(T-t+\frac{{\bf d}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{T-t}\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon},

where
U_{ot}
is defined in (
1.15
). Furthermore, if Assumption (A1) is replaced by the simpler (A1’), then the estimate can be improved to

|U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)-U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)|\leq C\left(1+\frac{{\bf d}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{(T-t)^{2}}\right)\varepsilon.

The above result is proved in Section
7
.

1.3.
Proof outline, methods, and a Corollary

To show Theorem
1.1
, unlike the classical approach for the finite dimensional problem (
1.7
), where one first proves qualitative convergence via the doubling of variables method and then derives a rate, we first extend the proof techniques developed in
[
TY21
]
to prove
u^{\varepsilon}
uniformly converges to a limit function with a quantitative rate
O(\varepsilon)
. Then, we use the doubling variable techniques to prove the limiting function must be a solution to the limiting equation (

HJB1

).

The main reason we are unable to work directly with (

HJB
ε

) is the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Even though, under our assumption from section
2
, we are able to show that the family
(U^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is uniformly equicontinuous (see Corollary
3.2
), in order to construct and characterize its limit via the doubling of variables method we require
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be locally compact– something that is not true. However, this difficulty can be overcome if someone knows apriori that
(U^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
converges uniformly to its limit; we refer to the main proof in subsection
5.2
and the comment before it.

To show the uniform convergence, as in the works
[
MTY19
,
TY21
]
mentioned above, our argument builds on the optimal control representation of
U^{\varepsilon}
(see (
1.1
)). In particular, we show that the control set
\mathcal{A}
in (
1.1
) can be extended without changing the value function and this new optimal control problem admits some equivalent representations that reveal its behavior as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The main steps are summarized below in the case of Theorem
1.1
. In what follows
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the initial time and distribution, respectively, and
H,L
are defined under the simplified assumption (A1’) described above (see also section
2
).

Step 1. (Extension of the control set)

We first extend the control set
\mathcal{A}
in (
1.1
) to

\mathcal{A}_{1}:=\left\{\alpha:[t,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}:\alpha\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\mathcal{F}\text{-measurable and }\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\alpha_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]<\infty\right\}

and we show that new value function
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
is in fact equal to
U^{\varepsilon}
(see Proposition
4.7
).

Step 2. (Alternative representation of the value function
U^{\varepsilon}
)

We derive an alternative formulation of
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
. Rather than working directly with the admissible controls
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and the stochastic trajectories
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
, with
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds
, we consider:

(1)

general target measures
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

(2)

random variables
X,Y\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathcal{L}(X)=m
and
\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu
, and

(3)

random paths
\gamma:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
joining
X
to
Y

5
5

5

In the sense that
\gamma\colon[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a measurable map such that
\gamma(\cdot,\omega)
is absolutely continuous for any
\omega\in\Omega
, and
\gamma(t,\omega)=X(\omega)
,
\gamma(T,\omega)=Y(\omega)
, for any
\omega\in\Omega
. We write
\gamma(t)=X
and
\gamma(T)=Y
for the last two equalities whenever there is no confusion.
,

U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
\mathcal{L}(X)=m,\;\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu\end{subarray}}\inf_{\gamma(t)=X,\;\gamma(T)=Y}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\omega}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s,\omega)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(s,\omega)\right)ds+G(\nu)\right]\right\}.

In this representation, we show that the last infimum can be exchanged with the expectation
\mathbb{E}
without changing the value (see Lemma
4.5
). After this, for each fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, the running cost inside the expectation becomes the minimal deterministic cost of transporting
x=X(\omega)
to
y=Y(\omega)
and is written as

(1.16)

h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,x,y):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\text{AC}([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
\gamma(t)=x,\;\gamma(T)=y\end{subarray}}\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(s)\right)ds,\;\;\text{for }x=X(\omega)\text{ and }y=Y(\omega).

More specifically,

(1.17)

U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu,\;\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu\end{subarray}}\left\{\mathbb{E}[h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X,Y)]+G(\nu)\right\}.

This running cost
h^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
was used in
[
TY21
]
to study the quantitative homogenization problem (
1.7
). A key technical ingredient in justifying the above formula is a uniform
L^{2}
velocity bound for nearly optimal paths
\gamma
, ensuring that the constant
C
in Theorem
1.1
depends only on the structural parameters of the problem.

Step 3. (Definition of the effective value function)

We introduce the limiting value function
\overline{U}
, in which the running cost is given by the expectation of the averaged cost
\overline{h}
associated with
h^{\varepsilon}
. Using the subadditivity and superadditivity of
h^{\varepsilon}
, we obtain both the uniform convergence
U^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\overline{U}
on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
the quantitative estimate
O(\varepsilon)
for the convergence rate. This step is proved in Theorem
5.2
.

Step 4. (Identification of the limit via the doubling variable method)

Finally, we use a doubling of variables argument to verify that
\overline{U}
is an
L
-viscosity solution of the effective Hamilton–Jacobi equation (

HJB1

). The uniqueness then yields
\overline{U}=U
, completing the proof of Theorem
1.1
. The proof of the viscosity property of
\overline{U}
is in subsection
5.2
and the proof of Theorem
1.1
is in subsection
5.3
.

Remark 1.4

(A Corollary of our proof methods)
.

(i) Even though, a posteriori, (
1.17
) is true for
U^{\varepsilon}
in place of
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
, we are unable to show it directly for
U^{\varepsilon}
using the outline in step 2 due to the controls in
\mathcal{A}
being progressively measurable.

(ii) It is worth noting that the optimal control problem can be equivalently written in the familiar Markovian form

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha(s,X_{s}),\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)ds+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right]\right\},

where the infimum is taken over all measurable
\alpha\colon[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
dX_{s}=\alpha(s,X_{s})ds,
for
s\in[t,T]
,
\int_{t}^{T}\mathbb{E}[|\alpha(s,X_{s})|^{2}]ds<\infty
and
X_{t}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
. We refer to
[
LSZ22
, Theorem 8.1]
or
[
CDJM25
, Lemma 6.2]
.

(iii) In order to show the equality in step 1, we compare the value
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
with the value functions of the optimal control problem (
1.1
) when the control set is extended (this is the case of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
above) or restricted in the sense that the controls
\alpha
are not just
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable but
\alpha(s,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for any
s\in[t,T]
(see
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in section
4.1
). In both cases we show that the value function remains the same (see section
4.3
) the main reason being that the randomness generated by the Brownian motion allows us to produce enough paths
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
and, in case
m
has atoms, for the mass to split. It is natural to consider the even further restricted control set

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\left\{\alpha\colon[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\;\alpha\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\sigma(X_{t})\text{-measurable}\right\},

where
\sigma(X_{t})
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable
X_{t}
, and compare it with the value function of (
1.1
). As a consequence of the methods developed in this paper, we obtain the following Corollary.

Corollary 1.5
.

Assume that (A1’)-(A2) are in place and
L
is defined as in (
1.3
). For
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define

\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},\alpha_{s}\right)ds+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right]\right\},

where
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds,\;s\in[t,T],\;\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
.
Then the following statements hold.

(1)
\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
, for any
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is non-atomic. As a result,
\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
converges to
U(t,m)
, where
U
is the unique solution of (

HJB1

), with rate
O(\varepsilon)
for any
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
non-atomic.

(2) Let
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Lipschitz, and let
u^{\varepsilon}
be the solution to (
1.7
).
Suppose that

G(m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\,m(dx),\qquad m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Then, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U^{\varepsilon}(t,\delta_{x})=\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,\delta_{x})=u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
.
Consequently, Theorem
1.1
implies that

\left|u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)-u(t,x)\right|\leq C\varepsilon,\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

recovering
[
TY21
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Remark 1.6
.

Note that in the setting of part
(2)
, with proper regularity assumptions, one can prove that
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)m(dx)
for any
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Formally, let
W(t,m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)m(dx)
. We notice
D_{m}W(t,m)=\nabla_{x}\frac{\delta W}{\delta m}=D_{x}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
. Because
u^{\varepsilon}
solves (
1.7
), we have

\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+H(x/\varepsilon,x,D_{x}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x))=0,\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Integrating
x
with
m
gives

\partial_{t}\int u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)m(dx)+\int H(x/\varepsilon,x,D_{x}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x))m(dx)=0\,.

The rigorous argument follows by generalizing the proof of Corollary
1.5

(2)
, where only the special case
m=\delta_{x_{0}}
is considered in this paper. More precisely, using the optimal control representation, one shows that
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)m(dx)
,
and the desired identity follows. Since this is not the main focus of our work, we omit the detailed proof for simplicity.

However, for general terminal costs
G
, as in part (1), the function
\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)
might not be continuous everywhere, hence the equality
\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}
is not expected to hold everywhere.
A case of strict inequality
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)<\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
, with
G(m)={\bf d}_{1}(m,\text{Leb}|_{[0,1]^{d}})
, was presented in Cecchin-Daudin-Jackson-Martini
[
CDJM25
, Corollary 6.10]
. We give the proof of Corollary
1.5
and an example of a discontinuous
\widetilde{U}^{\varepsilon}
in Appendix
B
.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
, which assumes the more general Assumption (A1), follows a similar strategy to that of multiscale problems in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see
[
HJ23
]
), with an additional difficulty arising from the need to handle the measurability of admissible paths and the dependence of the cost
L
on the law
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
. The main idea is to break the time period
[t,T]
into
N\in\mathbb{N}
evenly spaced subintervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}],\;i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
. For each subinterval, we fix the argument of the macroscopic variable
X_{s}
and the probability measure
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
at the value of initial time
t_{i}
, while retaining the dependence on
X_{s}/\varepsilon
. We may then apply the result of Theorem
1.1
for each subinterval and we also estimate the approximation error for fixing the macroscopic and measure arguments. The final step is to optimize with respect to
N
in order to minimize the error.

To prove Theorem
1.3
we first show that the functions
U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu),\;U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)
from (
1.4
) and (
1.15
), maintain their values if the control set
\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}
is extended to controls in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
so that the state process
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
, with
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds,\;\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu
, satisfies
\mathcal{L}(X_{T})=\nu
. This requires us to explore certain continuity properties of dynamic optimal transport problems
U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}
under different sets of controls. We then apply the techniques developed in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
to obtain the rate of convergence.

1.4.
Related literature and outlook

To the best of our knowledge, the quantitative homogenization results in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are new. Homogenization of first order HJ equations in infinite dimensions was studied from a qualitative point of view in Gangbo-Tudorascu
[
GT12
, Section 3.3]
, and in the recent
[
Par26
]
from a quantitative point of view, where, in the latter work, the main first-order HJ equation is posed in
L^{2}
and additional rearrangement and periodicity assumptions are made to compensate for the lack of compactness
6
6

6

We note that similar compactness properties can be obtained in our setting if
H(y,\cdot,p,\cdot),\;G(\cdot)
are apriori defined on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
instead of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and hence (

HJB
ε

) is posed in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
instead of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
.
An analogous qualitative version of Theorem
1.3
was obtained also in
[
GT12
]
, where the authors proved the
\Gamma
-convergence of the integral functional, and extended classical results (e.g. Weinan
[
Wei91
, Section 7]
) to infinite dimensions. To obtain a quantitative version of the result in
[
GT12
]
in the present paper, we use the quantitative convergence of transport cost functions proved in
[
TY21
]
. This result controls the convergence of the cost of transporting
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
with oscillatory running cost
L(x/\varepsilon,\cdot)
to the effective cost associated with the homogenized running cost
\overline{L}
. We combine this estimate with a suitable partition of the optimal transport path to handle the multiscale dependence and nonlinear dependence on the law (general mean-field interactions).

Explicit examples of effective Hamiltonians
\overline{H}
and effective Lagrangians
\overline{L}
are sparse in the literature. We refer to
[
Tra21
, Section 4]
for some of them. A computation verifying Theorem
1.3
has appeared in
[
GY25
, Section 5.2]
, where the homogenized optimal transport distance
U_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)
is compared with the metric corresponding to a homogenized gradient flow in the Wasserstein space.

A natural follow up question is whether the results presented in this paper can be extended to the case where the dynamics of
X_{s}
in (
1.1
) depend on the Brownian motion
B
(case with idiosyncratic noise), or on a common noise
B^{0}
(and the distributions in the cost functional are subject to the common noise), or both; we refer to
[
DPT22b
,
DPT22a
]
for the precise setup. In the presence of noise, questions of homogenization have only been studied in the periodic case where measures are defined on the compact space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and the PDE techniques from the theory of finite dimensional HJ equations are more easily accessible. For related singular perturbation problems involving second-order Hamilton–Jacobi equations in the Wasserstein space and coupled systems of controlled conditional slow-fast McKean–Vlasov SDEs, we refer to
[
Zit25
,
Zit23
]
. To our knowledge, our proof methods, especially the formulations developed in section
4
(and allowed us to circumvent the difficulties created by the lack of local compactness of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) cannot be directly applied to the case where there is idiosyncratic or common noise. We intend to investigate this extension in a future work.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In section 2, we state our assumptions on
H,\;G
and some preliminary results about the effective Hamiltonian
\overline{H}
and its dual
\overline{L}
defined in (
1.14
). In section 3, we discuss the definition of
L
-viscosity solutions for (

HJB
ε

), (

HJB1

) and (

HJB2

), as well as a relation between these equations and their finite dimensional counterparts. In section 4, we present and prove the equivalent formulations of
U^{\varepsilon}
from (
1.1
). In section 5, we study the behavior of
U^{\varepsilon}
under the stronger Assumption (A1’) (Theorem
1.1
). In section 6, we prove Theorem
1.2
. Section 7 is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.3
. Finally, we use the appendix to show some technical propositions and Corollary
1.5
.

2.
Notation, Assumptions and Preliminary Results

2.1.
Notation

Throughout the paper, for
2\geq p\geq 1
, we use the notation
\mathcal{P}_{p}=\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the space of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moment endowed with the
{\bf d}_{p}
Wasserstein metric, which is given by

{\bf d}_{p}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}\pi(dx,dy)\right)^{1/p},\;\;\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p},

where
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is the set of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with marginals
\mu
and
\nu
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
is the Dirac delta measure at
x
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
, we denote by
m^{N}_{\bm{x}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
the empirical measure.
|\cdot|
is the usual euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\|\cdot\|_{p}
the
p
-norm; that is
\|\cdot\|_{2}=|\cdot|
. For
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
we use the symbol
\mathcal{B}(A)
for the Borel sigma algebra over
A
. For a path
\gamma:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\gamma_{s}:=\gamma(s,\cdot)
the associated random variable at time
s
. Given a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we use the symbol
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the inner product and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for the norm.

For the rest of the paper,
\mathbb{E}
or
\mathbb{E}_{\omega}
will be the expectation defined for the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
. For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we also use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]=\int f(x)dx
. For a random variable
X\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
X\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
we write
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
for its law or simply
X\sim\mu
. If the random variables
X,Y
have the same law, we write
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Y
. We also write
\sigma(X,Y)
for the sigma algebra generated by the random variables
X,Y
.

For functions
u(t,x)=u\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\dot{u}
or
\partial_{t}u
its time derivative and by
D_{x}u
or simply
Du
(whenever there is no confusion) its spacial derivative.

2.2.
Assumptions

We impose the following assumptions on the Hamiltonian
H
and the terminal cost
G
. Let
r\in(0,1)
be given.

(A1) Let
H:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. There exist constants
C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{H}>0
such that :

(2.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378ptH(y,x,p,m)\text{ is convex in }p,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826ptC_{1,H}|p|^{2}-K_{0}\leq H(y,x,p,m)\leq C_{2,H}|p|^{2}+K_{0},

(2.3)

\displaystyle|H(y,x,p,m)-H(y^{\prime},x^{\prime},p^{\prime},m^{\prime})|\leq C_{H}(1+|p|+|p^{\prime}|)(|x-x^{\prime}|+|y-y^{\prime}|+|p-p^{\prime}|+{\bf d}_{2-r}(m,m^{\prime})),

for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d},x,x^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

(A2) The terminal cost
G:\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is bounded and
{\bf d}_{2-r}
-Lipschitz; that is, there exists a constant
C_{G}
such that

(2.4)

|G(m)|\leq C_{G}\quad\text{and}\quad|G(m_{1})-G(m_{2})|\leq C_{G}{\bf d}_{2-r}(m_{1},m_{2}),\text{ for every }m,m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}.

To establish the convergence rate of
U^{\varepsilon}
to
U
under these assumptions, we first consider a simplified setting in which the Hamiltonian does not depend (or is constant) on the macroscopic variable
x
and the probability measure
m
. In particular, in Section
5
we replace (A1) with the reduced assumption (A1’) to obtain quantitative convergence results in this case, and then return to the full assumption (A1) in Section
6
.

(A1’) The Hamiltonian
H:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies the following assumptions:

(2.5)

\displaystyle H(y,p)

\displaystyle\text{ is convex in }p,

(2.6)

\displaystyle C_{1,H}|p|^{2}-K_{0}

\displaystyle\leq H(y,p)\leq C_{2,H}|p|^{2}+K_{0},\;\text{ for some constants }C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0}>0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle|H(y,p)-H(y^{\prime},p^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{H}(1+|p|+|p^{\prime}|)(|y-y^{\prime}|+|p-p^{\prime}|),

for some constants
C_{H}>0
and for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;,p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The assumptions imposed on
H,G
guarantee the existence and the uniqueness of an
L
-viscosity solution of (

HJB
ε

). We refer to section
3
for a more detailed discussion about the definition, the solution and its properties.

2.3.
Preliminary Results

2.3.1.
Properties of Lagrangians

Under (A1) and (A2), we collect several properties of
L
, the Legendre transform of
H
(defined in (
1.3
)), the effective Hamiltonian
\overline{H}
, and its Legendre transform
\overline{L}
. The proofs of Propositions
2.1
and
2.2
are given in Appendix
A
, while the proof of Proposition
2.3
is similar and therefore omitted. The same properties still hold if (A1) is replaced by (A1’), that is, when
H(y,x,p,m)
is independent of the variables
x
and
m
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
L:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the Legendre tranform of
H
, defined by

L(y,x,v,m):=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{-p\cdot v-H(y,x,p,m)\big\}.

Then, the following statements hold.

\rm{(1)}

L
is finite everywhere on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

\rm{(2)}

For any
y\in\mathbb{T}^{d},x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, the map
v\mapsto L(y,x,v,m)
is convex.

\rm{(3)}

For any
y\in\mathbb{T}^{d},x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(2.8)

\frac{|v|^{2}}{4C_{2,H}}-K_{0}\leq L(y,x,v,m)\leq\frac{|v|^{2}}{4C_{1,H}}+K_{0}.

\rm{(4)}

There exists a constant
C_{L}=C_{L}(C_{1,H},C_{H},K_{0})>0
such that for all

y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(2.9)

|L(y,x,v,m)-L(y^{\prime},x^{\prime},v^{\prime},m^{\prime})|\leq C_{L}(1+|v|+|v^{\prime}|)(|x-x^{\prime}|+|y-y^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|+{\bf d}_{2}(m,m^{\prime})).

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\delta>0
. Fix
x,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Let
v^{\delta}=v^{\delta}(\cdot,x,p,m)\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

denote the unique viscosity solution of the discounted cell problem

(2.10)

\delta v^{\delta}(y)+H\big(y,x,Dv^{\delta}(y)+p,m\big)=0,\qquad y\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
p
and
H
, such that

\delta\|v^{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\|Dv^{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C.

Moreover, as
\delta\to 0^{+}
, the family
\delta v^{\delta}(\cdot,x,p,m)
converges uniformly on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
to a constant
-\overline{H}(x,p,m)
. Finally, allowing
x,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
to vary, the function
\overline{H}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the following growth and regularity estimates: there exists a constant
C_{\overline{H}}=C_{\overline{H}}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0})>0
such that

(2.11)

\displaystyle\overline{H}(x,p,m)

\displaystyle\text{ is convex in }p,

(2.12)

\displaystyle C_{1,H}|p|^{2}-K_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{H}(x,p,m)\leq C_{2,H}|p|^{2}+K_{0},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\big|\overline{H}(x,p,m)-\overline{H}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime},m^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\overline{H}}\big(1+|p|+|p^{\prime}|\big)\left(|x-x^{\prime}|+|p-p^{\prime}|+{\bf d}_{2}(m,m^{\prime})\right),

for all
x,x^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, where
C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0}>0
are the constants appearing in
(
2.2
) and (
2.3
).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\overline{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the Legendre transform of
\overline{H}
, defined in (
1.14
) by

\overline{L}(x,v,m)=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{-p\cdot v-\overline{H}(x,p,m)\big\}.

Then, the following statements hold.

\rm{(1)}

\overline{L}
is finite everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

\rm{(2)}

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, the map
v\mapsto\overline{L}(x,v,m)
is convex.

\rm{(3)}

For any
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(2.14)

\frac{|v|^{2}}{4C_{2,H}}-K_{0}\leq\overline{L}(x,v,m)\leq\frac{|v|^{2}}{4C_{1,H}}+K_{0}.

\rm{(4)}

There exists a constant
C_{\overline{L}}=C_{\overline{L}}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0})>0
such that for all

x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(2.15)

\left|\overline{L}(x,v,m)-\overline{L}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime},m^{\prime})\right|\leq C_{\overline{L}}(1+|v|+|v^{\prime}|)(|x-x^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|+{\bf d}_{2}(m,m^{\prime})).

2.3.2.
Properties of the cost function
h^{\varepsilon}

To study the convergence rates later, we introduce the cost function
h^{\varepsilon}
associated with the Lagrangian
L
. Under Assumption (A1’),
h^{\varepsilon}
was given in (
1.16
) and represents the minimal action required to travel from
x
to
y
over the time interval
[t,T]
. We give the following more general definition for the time interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]
.

Definition 2.4
.

(1) Assume (A1). For
\varepsilon>0
, define
h^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\times[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(2.16)

h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in{\rm AC}\left([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)\\
\gamma(t_{1})=x,\gamma(t_{2})=y\end{subarray}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}(t),\mu\right)dt.

For
\varepsilon=1
, we write

(2.17)

h(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathrm{AC}\left([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)\\
\gamma(t_{1})=x,\gamma(t_{2})=y\end{subarray}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\gamma(t),c,\dot{\gamma}(t),\mu\right)dt.

(2) Assume (A1’). For
\varepsilon>0
, define
h^{\varepsilon}:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(2.18)

h^{\varepsilon}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in{\rm AC}\left([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)\\
\gamma(t_{1})=x,\gamma(t_{2})=y\end{subarray}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma(t)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(t)\right)dt.

For
\varepsilon=1
, we write

(2.19)

h(t_{1},t_{2},x,y)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathrm{AC}\left([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)\\
\gamma(t_{1})=x,\gamma(t_{2})=y\end{subarray}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)\right)dt.

In both cases, for
\varepsilon>0
, we denote by
h^{\varepsilon}
the minimal action
associated with the Lagrangian
L
. When
\varepsilon=1
, the cost function
h

coincides with the cost function
m
introduced in
[
TY21
]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

(1) Assume (A1) and (A2). Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
Then the map
(c,x,y)\mapsto h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In particular, it is Borel measurable.

(2) Assume (A1’) and (A2). Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
. Then the map
(x,y)\mapsto h^{\varepsilon}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular, it is Borel measurable.

Proof.

We only prove part (1), as the proof of part (2) is analogous.

Let
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exists a minimizer
\gamma^{\ast}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)
(see
[
Tra21
, Lemma D.2]
,
[
CS04
]
), that is,

\gamma^{\ast}\in\operatorname*{argmin}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathrm{AC}\bigl([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\bigr)\\
\gamma(t_{1})=x,\ \gamma(t_{2})=y\end{subarray}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}(t),\mu\right)dt.

Consider the path
\eta:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by
\displaystyle\eta(t)=x+\frac{t-t_{1}}{t_{2}-t_{1}}\left(y-x\right)
. By the definition of
h^{\varepsilon}
and (
2.8
),

(2.20)

h^{\varepsilon}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}L\left(\frac{\eta(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\eta}(t),\mu\right)dt\leq\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{4C_{1,H}(t_{2}-t_{1})}+K_{0}\left(t_{2}-t_{1}\right).

On the other hand, again by (
2.8
),

h^{\varepsilon}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)dt\geq\frac{1}{4C_{2,H}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|^{2}\,dt-K_{0}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

Hence,

(2.21)

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|^{2}\,dt\leq\frac{C_{2,H}|x-y|^{2}}{C_{1,H}(t_{2}-t_{1})}+8C_{2,H}K_{0}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

Let
\delta=\frac{t_{2}-t_{1}}{4}
. Define
\tilde{\gamma}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\tilde{\gamma}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\gamma^{\ast}(t)+\frac{\delta+t_{1}-t}{\delta}\left(x^{\prime}-x\right),\quad\,\,\,\text{ if }t\in\left[t_{1},t_{1}+\delta\right],\\
&\gamma^{\ast}(t),\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{ if }t\in\left[t_{1}+\delta,t_{2}-\delta\right],\\
&\gamma^{\ast}(t)+\frac{t-\left(t_{2}-\delta\right)}{\delta}\left(y^{\prime}-y\right),\quad\text{ if }t\in\left[t_{2}-\delta,t_{2}\right].\end{aligned}\right.

By construction,
\tilde{\gamma}
is admissible for
h^{\varepsilon}(c^{\prime},\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle h^{\varepsilon}(c^{\prime},\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}L\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}(t)}{\varepsilon},c^{\prime},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}(t),\mu\right)\,dt-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III},

where

\mathrm{I}:=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{1}+\delta}\Bigg|L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)+\frac{\delta+t_{1}-t}{\delta}(x^{\prime}-x)}{\varepsilon},c^{\prime},\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)-\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{\delta},\mu\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)\Bigg|\,dt,

\mathrm{II}:=\int_{t_{2}-\delta}^{t_{2}}\Bigg|L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)+\frac{t-(t_{2}-\delta)}{\delta}(y^{\prime}-y)}{\varepsilon},c^{\prime},\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)+\frac{y^{\prime}-y}{\delta},\mu\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)\Bigg|\,dt,

and

\mathrm{III}:=\int_{t_{1}+\delta}^{t_{2}-\delta}\Bigg|L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c^{\prime},\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\ast}(t)}{\varepsilon},c,\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t),\mu\right)\Bigg|\,dt.

By (
2.9
) and (
2.21
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{1}+\delta}\left(1+\frac{|x^{\prime}-x|}{\delta}+2\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(\delta+|x^{\prime}-x|+2\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{1}+\delta}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(1+\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+|x-y|\right),

for some constant
C=C(\varepsilon,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0},T-t_{1})>0
.
Similarly,

\mathrm{II}\leq C\left(\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(1+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|x-y|\right).

Again by (
2.9
) and (
2.21
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}|c^{\prime}-c|\int_{t_{1}+\delta}^{t_{2}-\delta}\left(1+2\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|\right)\,dt\leq C_{L}|c^{\prime}-c|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left(1+2\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}|c^{\prime}-c|\left((t_{2}-t_{1})+2\left(\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\ast}(t)\right|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C|c^{\prime}-c|\left(1+|x-y|\right),

for some constant
C=C(\varepsilon,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0},t_{2}-t_{1})>0
.
Combining these estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle h^{\varepsilon}(c^{\prime},\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(1+\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|x-y|\right).

By symmetry, exchanging
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
gives

\displaystyle h^{\varepsilon}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)-h^{\varepsilon}\left(c^{\prime},\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(1+\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\right).

Therefore,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\left|h^{\varepsilon}(c^{\prime},\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|c^{\prime}-c|\right)\left(1+\left|x^{\prime}-x\right|+\left|y^{\prime}-y\right|+|x-y|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\right),

where
C=C(\varepsilon,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{H},K_{0},t_{2}-t_{1})>0
. In particular, the map
(c,x,y)\mapsto h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)
is continuous, and hence measurable.
∎

For the homogenization problem in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
considered in
[
TY21
]
, the quantitative convergence argument relies on establishing both the subadditivity and the superadditivity of the cost function
h
. Subadditivity, together with Fekete’s lemma, implies the existence of the averaged metric
\overline{h}
. Combining subadditivity and superadditivity with
\overline{h}
then yields an
O(\varepsilon)
rate of convergence. These results are summarized in the following proposition, which we will use to establish the uniform convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
to its limit, as well as the corresponding rate of convergence.

Proposition 2.6

(Restatement of
[
TY21
]
, Lemma 3.1, 3.2)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
.

(1) (Subadditivity) For any
k,l>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
independent of
t_{1},t_{2},x,y
such that

\displaystyle h\bigl((k+l)t_{1},(k+l)t_{2},(k+l)x,(k+l)y\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq h\left(kt_{1},kt_{2},kx,ky\right)+h\left(lt_{1},lt_{2},lx,ly\right)+C\left(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}\right).

For the case
k=l=1
, we have

h\bigl(2t_{1},2t_{2},2x,2y\bigr)\leq 2h\bigl(t_{1},t_{2},x,y\bigr)+C\left(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}\right).

(2) (Superadditivity) There exists a constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
independent of
t_{1},t_{2},x,y
such that

2h\bigl(t_{1},t_{2},x,y\bigr)\leq h\bigl(2t_{1},2t_{2},2x,2y\bigr)+C\left(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}\right).

(3) The limit

\overline{h}\left(t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon h\left(\frac{t_{1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{t_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}\right)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}h^{\varepsilon}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y)

exists, and

\left|\overline{h}\left(t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)-h^{\varepsilon}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y)\right|\leq C\left(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}\right)\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
independent of
t_{1},t_{2},x,y
.

For the reader’s convenience, we provide proofs of
\rm{(1)}
and
\rm{(3)}
in Appendix
A
. The proof of
\rm{(2)}
can be found in
[
TY21
]
.

3.
Solutions to 1st order HJ equations in the Wasserstein Space

In this section, we provide the definition of
L
-viscosity solutions of (

HJB
ε

) and we prove some regularity properties that they possess. As mentioned in the introduction, apart from being the dynamic programming equations to stochastic optimal control problems, Hamilton–Jacobi equations in the Wasserstein space of the form (

HJB
ε

) arise from finite dimensional problems of the form

(
\mathrm{HJB}_{N}
)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}V^{N}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}H(x^{i}/\varepsilon,x^{i},ND_{x^{i}}V^{N}_{\varepsilon},m^{N}_{\bm{x}})=0,&(t,\bm{x})\in(0,+\infty)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N},\\
V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(T,\bm{x})=G(m_{\bm{x}}^{N}),&\bm{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N},\end{cases}

where
N\in\mathbb{N}
, as
N\to+\infty
. The latter equation is understood in the viscosity sense (see
[
CIL92
]
). Given a solution
V^{N}_{\varepsilon}
of (

HJB
N

), we may establish uniform equicontinuity for the sequence
(V^{N}_{\varepsilon})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume that (A1) and (A2) hold. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider
V^{N}_{\varepsilon}
the unique viscosity solution of (

HJB
N

) with
H
substituted by
H^{\varepsilon}(x,y,p,m)=H\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},y,p,m\right)
. There exists a constant
C_{0}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
and a constant
C>0
, which might depend on
\varepsilon
, such that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the unique viscosity solution of (

HJB
N

) satisfies

(3.1)

\displaystyle|V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bm{x})-V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(s,\bm{y})|\leq C_{0}\left({\bf d}_{2}(m_{\bm{x}}^{N},m_{\bm{y}}^{N})+|t-s|\right),\;\;\text{and}

(3.2)

\displaystyle|V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bm{x})-V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(s,\bm{y})|\leq C\left({\bf d}_{2-r}(m_{\bm{x}}^{N},m_{\bm{y}}^{N})+|t-s|\right),

for all
t,s\in[0,T]
and
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
.

Proof.

We give the proof in Appendix
C
.
∎

We note that analogous regularity estimates when
\varepsilon=1
, that is when
H^{\varepsilon}=H
, can be found in
[
GMS21
, Theorem 3.3]
or
[
DJS25a
, Proposition 2.5]
.

To find the relation between a solution of (

HJB
N

) and a solution of (

HJB
ε

), one exploits the uniform equicontinuity of
(V^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
provided by Proposition
3.1
, to show that the family of functions
(U^{N}_{\varepsilon})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
U^{N}_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2-r}\to\mathbb{R}
and

(3.3)

U^{N}_{\varepsilon}(t,m):=\inf_{\bm{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}}\left\{V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bm{x})+C{\bf d}_{2-r}(m^{N}_{\bm{x}},m)\right\},

where
C
is the constant in (
3.2
), is uniformly equicontinuous
7
7

7

In particular,
U^{N}_{\varepsilon}
is
{\bf d}_{2-r}
–Lipschitz with constant
C
.
and equibounded as a sequence in
N
, since
(V_{\varepsilon}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is equibounded. Therefore, we can apply the Arzela–Ascoli theorem on the relatively compact subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{2-r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

(3.4)

M_{R}^{2}=\left\{m\in\mathcal{P}_{2-r}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}m(dx)\leq R\right\},\;R>0,

to derive that (up to a subsequence)
U^{N}_{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly in
[0,T]\times M_{R}^{2}
to a function
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is also
{\bf d}_{2-r}
-Lipchitz with constant
C
.
For
\varepsilon=1
, it was shown
in
[
GMS21
, Theorem 1.2]
under assumptions (A1), (A2), that for any bounded
B\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}
we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{(t,m_{\bm{x}}^{N})\in[0,T]\times B}|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m_{\bm{x}}^{N})-V^{N}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bm{x})|=0,

and, furthermore, the limit
U^{\varepsilon}
satisfies (

HJB
ε

) in the “lifted” viscosity sense of P.L Lions (
L
-viscosity solutions).

Before introducing the definition of
L
-viscosity solutions, we collect the results of the above discussion together with a uniform in
\varepsilon

{\bf d}_{2}
-Lipschitz estimate for
U^{\varepsilon}
. We remark that we do not get a uniform in
\varepsilon

{\bf d}_{2-r}
-Lipschitz estimate as the constant
C
in (
3.2
) from Proposition
3.1
depends on
\varepsilon
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Assume that (A1) and (A2) are in place and
\varepsilon>0
is fixed. Let
U^{N}_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2-r}\to\mathbb{R}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, be as in (
3.3
). Then, the sequence
(U^{N}_{\varepsilon})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges (up to a subsequence) uniformly in the sets
[0,T]\times M_{R}^{2}
, where
M_{R}^{2}
is as in (
3.4
), to a function
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. The function
U^{\varepsilon}
is an
L
-viscosity solution of (

HJB
ε

) and, furthermore,

(3.5)

|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m_{1})-U^{\varepsilon}(s,m_{2})|\leq C_{0}\left(|t-s|+{\bf d}_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})\right),

for all
t,s\in[0,T]
and
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, where
C_{0}
is the constant from (
3.1
).

Proof.

We give the proof in Appendix
C
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

The above estimate is the infinite dimensional analogue of the equi-Lipschitz property of the family
(u^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
, where
u^{\varepsilon}
solves (
1.7
), obtained in
[
LPV87
]
. In addition, we note that the results of Proposition
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
hold even without the convexity assumption on the map
p\mapsto H(y,x,p,m)
.

3.1.
L
-viscosity solutions

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a non-atomic probability space. We consider
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is a separable Hilbert space endowed with the inner product
\langle X,Y\rangle=\mathbb{E}[XY]
,
X,Y\in\mathcal{H}
. Its norm is denoted by
\|X\|_{2}=\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\right)^{1/2}
.

Definition 3.4
.

For a given
\Phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\widehat{\Phi}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
its “lift” defined by

\widehat{\Phi}(X)=\Phi(\mathcal{L}(X)).

Remark 3.5
.

Recall that for a function
\widehat{\Phi}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
its gradient
D\widehat{\Phi}(X)\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies

\widehat{\Phi}(X+Y)=\widehat{\Phi}(X)+\langle D\widehat{\Phi}(X),Y\rangle+o(\|Y\|_{2}),\;\;X,Y\in\mathcal{H}.

If
\Phi
is smooth enough (see
[
CD+18
]
), the gradient
D\widehat{\Phi}
is related to the Wasserstein derivative
D_{m}\Phi
in the following way

D_{m}\Phi(\mathcal{L}(X),X)=D\widehat{\Phi}(X),\;\;X\in\mathcal{H}.

With this in mind, we notice that, at least formally, if
U
satisfies (

HJB
ε

), then its “lift”
\widehat{U}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies the lifted equation

(
\mathrm{HJB}_{\mathcal{H}}
)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}\widehat{U}+\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(X,D\widehat{U}(t,X))=0,&(t,X)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{H},\\
\widehat{U}(T,X)=\widehat{G}(X),&X\in\mathcal{H},\end{cases}

where

\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(X,P)=\mathbb{E}[H(X/\varepsilon,X,P,\mathcal{L}(X))],\quad\widehat{G}(X)=G(\mathcal{L}(X)),\;\;\text{for }X,P\in\mathcal{H}.

Definition 3.6
.

An upper semicontinuous (resp. lower semicontinuous) function
V:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a viscosity subsolution (resp. supersolution) of (

HJB
H

) if

•

V(T,X)\leq(\text{resp. }\geq)\;\widehat{G}(X)
;

•

For any
\Phi\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{H})
and each
(t_{0},X_{0})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{H}
such that
V(t_{0},X_{0})-\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})
has a local maximum (resp. local minimum), we have

-\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})+\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(X_{0},D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0}))\leq(\text{resp. }\geq)\;0.

V
is a viscosity solution of (

HJB
H

) if it is a viscosity subsolution and a viscosity supersolution.

Definition 3.7
.

A function
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a viscosity solution/subsolution/supersolution of (

HJB
ε

) if its lift
\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}
is a viscosity solution/subsolution/supersolution of (

HJB
H

).

From now on, when refering to viscosity solutions of (

HJB1

) or (

HJB
ε

), we mean in the sense of Definition
3.7
.
It was proved in
[
GMS21
, Theorem 7.4]
that there is comparison between bounded viscosity sub and super solutions of (

HJB
H

) given our assumptions (A1) and (A2). Furthermore, using the dynamic programming principle from
[
DPT22b
]
, it can be shown that the value function of the mean field control problem (
1.1
) is a viscosity solution of (

HJB
ε

). We record this in the following Theorem.

Theorem 3.8
.

Assume that
H,G
satisfy assumptions (A1)-(A2) or (A1’)-(A2). Then, (

HJB
ε

) has a unique
L
-viscosity solution
U^{\varepsilon}
which is given by (
1.1
) for
L
as in (
1.3
). Furthermore, if
\overline{H}
is the corresponding effective Hamiltonian constructed in Proposition
2.2
, the equations (

HJB1

) and (

HJB2

) have unique
L
-viscosity solutions.

We finish the section with the following remark.

Remark 3.9
.

(i) Assume that
\varepsilon=1
. If
U:=U^{\varepsilon=1}
is a classical solution of (

HJB
ε

), that is the Wasserstein
D_{m}U
and time
\partial_{t}U
derivatives exist and (

HJB
ε

) holds everywhere, then we can verify that
U(t,m_{\bm{x}}^{N})=:u^{N}(t,\bm{x})
is a classical solution of (

HJB
N

). Assuming uniqueness of solutions of (

HJB
N

), it follows that
u^{N}(t,\bm{x})=V^{N}(t,\bm{x})
.

(ii) The PDE (

HJB
ε

) does not always admit a classical solution. For example, if
H(y,x,p,m)=\frac{|p|^{2}}{2}
and
G(m)=g(\int xm(dx))
for some
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the L-viscosity solution of (

HJB
ε

) is
U(t,m)=u(t,\int xm(dx))
, where
u
is the unique viscosity solution of

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}u+\frac{1}{2}|Du|^{2}=0,&(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(T,x)=g(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

which does not have classical solutions in general. The proof of this fact is similar to
[
DDJ24
, Proposition 2.10]
which uses the optimal control formulation of the solution.

(iii) By considering periodic data, the construction presented in this section can be transferred to
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(instead of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

4.
Different types of admissible controls

4.1.
Alternative control sets

In this section, we introduce several classes of admissible controls that will be used to establish the rate of convergence.

Recall that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
is a sufficiently rich, atomless filtered probability space, and let
B
be an
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion. Let
\xi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Gaussian random variable, independent of
B
, such that
\sigma(\xi)\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
T>0
. For fixed
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
, consider the control problem

(4.1)

\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right\},

where
\mathcal{A}=\left\{\alpha:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\alpha\text{ is }\mathbb{F}\text{-progressively measurable and }\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\alpha_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]<\infty\right\}
. For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and each
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable

X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
, independent of
B
, consider the controlled process
X
that satisfies
X_{s}(\omega)=X_{t}(\omega)+\int_{t}^{s}\alpha(r,\omega)dr
a.s.. The associated value function is
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
.

We now introduce two alternative admissible control sets.

(1) Define

\mathcal{A}_{1}:=\left\{\alpha:[t,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}:\alpha\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\mathcal{F}\text{-measurable and }\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\alpha_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]<\infty\right\}.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and each
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable

X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
, independent of
B
,
consider the controlled process
X
as above. The corresponding value function in (
4.1
) associated with
\mathcal{A}_{1}

is denoted by
U_{1}(t,m)
.

With this choice of controls, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can generate any coupling
\pi\in\Pi(m,\nu)
as
\pi=\mathcal{L}((X_{t},X_{T}))
.

(2) Define

\mathcal{A}_{2}:=\left\{\alpha:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\;\left|\;\begin{aligned} &\alpha_{s}(\omega)=\tilde{\alpha}\left(s,\xi(\omega)\right),\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\alpha_{s}\rvert^{2}\,ds\right]<\infty,\\
&\tilde{\alpha}:[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ Borel measurable},\\
\end{aligned}\right.\right\}.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
and each
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable

X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
, independent of
B
,
consider the controlled process
X
as above. Denote the corresponding value function by
U^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,m)
.

In this setting, admissible controls are measurable with respect to the independent Gaussian variable
\xi
. This additional randomness allows the control to split mass. Our aim is to prove that
U^{\varepsilon}=U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
. However, a direct comparison is difficult because progressively measurable controls are technically delicate to handle. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce the intermediate value function
U_{2}^{\varepsilon}
, which provides a more tractable formulation and simplifies the proof. This is a randomization technique.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{1}\subset\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{A}_{2}
, it follows that

U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)\leq U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\leq U^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,m).

We will prove that under assumptions (A1) and (A2),
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}=U_{2}^{\varepsilon}
. Before proving this, we first provide equivalent formulations of
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
, which will be useful in the subsequent analysis.

4.2.
Equivalent formulations of
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}

To derive the equivalent formulations of
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
, we first define two admissible sets. Let
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

(1)

For any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we define

\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}:=\left\{(X,Y)\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\mathcal{L}(X)=m,\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu\right\}.

(2)

For
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}
, we define the set of admissible paths as

(4.2)

\Gamma(X,Y)=\left\{\,\gamma:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\mathcal{F}\text{-measurable},\\[4.0pt]
\gamma(\cdot,\omega)\in\text{AC}([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})\,\,\,\forall\,\omega\in\Omega,\\[4.0pt]
\gamma(t,\cdot)=X,\quad\gamma(T,\cdot)=Y\end{array}\right\}.

Then, we define a new value function
\widehat{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Define
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(4.3)

\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m):=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}}\left\{\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\right\}+G(\nu)\right\},

where
\gamma_{s}:=\gamma(s,\cdot)
.

Although the definitions of
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\tilde{U}^{\varepsilon}_{1}
seem very different, we will prove the equivalence of these two definitions, meaning
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}=\tilde{U}^{\varepsilon}_{1}
. The key observation is that the value functions of the optimal control problem in both (
4.1
) and (
4.3
) are functions of the optimal path. Thus, as long as both formulations allow similar sets of admissible paths, the optimal value is the same.

Before proving the equivalence between
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\tilde{U}^{\varepsilon}_{1}
, we establish a velocity bound for admissible paths
\gamma
. This estimate shows that, for nearly optimal choices of
\nu
and
(X,Y)
, the expected squared distance between
X
and
Y
is controlled by
(T-t)^{2}
. In particular, if
\gamma^{\delta}
is a
\delta
-optimal path with
0<\delta<T-t
, then the expected
L^{2}
-norm of its velocity is bounded.

Proposition 4.2
.

(1) For any
t\in[0,T]
and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

(4.4)

\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\left\{\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\right\}+G(\nu)\right\},

where

(4.5)

\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}=\left\{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}:\mathbb{E}\left|X-Y\right|^{2}\leq M_{0}(T-t)^{2}\right\},

for some constant
M_{0}=M_{0}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
that is independent of
m,\nu,X,Y,t
, and
T
.

(2) Let
t\in[0,T)
and
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
. Suppose
(\nu_{\delta},X^{\delta},Y^{\delta},\gamma^{\delta})
is a
\delta
-minimizer for
\tilde{U}^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)
, that is

(4.6)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma^{\delta}_{s},\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma^{\delta}_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu_{\delta})\leq\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

Then there exists a constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t).

Remark 4.3
.

In the second part of Proposition
4.2
,
\delta<T-t
is a technical assumption to ensure
M_{1}
is a constant independent of
\delta
. This condition can always be satisfied when we consider a path that is nearly optimal.

Proof.

If
t=T
, then by the definition of
\Gamma(X,Y)
in (
4.2
), we have
\gamma(t,\omega)=\gamma(T,\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Consequently, the admissible pair
(X,Y)
must satisfy
X=Y
, and the only admissible measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
is
\nu=m
. Hence,
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)=G(m)
. The same conclusion holds for the value function defined on the right-hand side of (
4.4
). Hence, (
4.4
) is valid when
t=T
.

Suppose
t<T
. By (
4.3
), choosing
\nu=m,(X,X)\in\mathcal{E}_{m,m}
,
\gamma(s,\cdot)=X
for all
s\in[t,T]
, we obtain

(4.7)

\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int^{T}_{t}L\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},x,0,m\right)\,ds\,m(dx)+G(m)\leq K_{0}(T-t)+G(m),

where the second inequality follows from (
2.8
).
Let
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
. Then there exist
\nu_{\delta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X^{\delta},Y^{\delta})\in\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu_{\delta}},\gamma^{\delta}\in\Gamma(X^{\delta},Y^{\delta})
such that
\gamma^{\delta}\left(t,\cdot\right)=X^{\delta}
,
\gamma^{\delta}\left(T,\cdot\right)=Y^{\delta}
, and
(
4.6
) holds. By (
2.8
),

(4.8)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma^{\delta}_{s},\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma^{\delta}_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\geq\frac{1}{4C_{2,H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]-K_{0}(T-t).

Combining (
4.7
), (
4.6
), (
4.8
), we obtain

\frac{1}{4C_{2,H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq(2K_{0}+1)(T-t)+G(m)-G(\nu_{\delta}).

Using (
2.4
), this implies,

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{2,H}(2K_{0}+1)(T-t)+4C_{2,H}C_{G}{\bf d}_{2}(m,\nu_{\delta})

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{2,H}(2K_{0}+1)(T-t)+4C_{2,H}C_{G}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|X^{\delta}-Y^{\delta}\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By Hölder’s inequality, for each
\omega\in\Omega
,

\left|X^{\delta}(\omega)-Y^{\delta}(\omega)\right|\leq\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}(t,\omega)\right|ds\leq\left(\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}(t,\omega)\right|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(T-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Combining this estimate with (
4.9
) yields

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|X^{\delta}-Y^{\delta}\right|^{2}}{(T-t)}\leq 4C_{2,H}(2K_{0}+1)(T-t)+4C_{2,H}C_{G}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|X^{\delta}-Y^{\delta}\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which implies

(4.10)

\mathbb{E}\left|X^{\delta}-Y^{\delta}\right|^{2}\leq M_{0}(T-t)^{2},

for some constant
M_{0}=M_{0}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
. Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, part (1) follows.

Finally, (
4.9
) and (
4.10
