) imply that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t),

for some constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0},M_{0})=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
, which completes the proof of part (2).
∎

We now prove the equivalence of
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume (A1) and (A2), and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}=\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Step 1: We show
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

Let
\delta>0
. Then there exist
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and an
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable
X_{t}
independent of
B
, with
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=m
, such that for the process
X_{s}
that satisfies
X_{s}=X_{t}+\int_{t}^{s}\alpha_{r}\,dr
a.s.,

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

Define
\gamma:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\gamma(s,\omega)=X_{t}(\omega)+\int_{t}^{s}\alpha(r,\omega)\,ds
, which is
\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\mathcal{F}
-measurable. For each
\omega\in\Omega
,
\gamma(\cdot,\omega)\in\mathrm{AC}([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\dot{\gamma}(s,\omega)=\alpha(s,\omega)
for
s\in[t,T]
. Set
X:=X_{t}
,
Y:=X_{T}
, and
\nu:=\mathcal{L}(X_{T})
. Note that
X,Y\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F};\mathbb{R}^{d})
since
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.Therefore,
(\nu,X,Y,\gamma)
belong to the admissible sets of
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)
in (
4.3
), and

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

Letting
\delta\to 0
yields
\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

Step 2: We show
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}\leq\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

If
t=T
, then
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)=G(m)=\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)
.

Suppose
t<T
. Let
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
. Then there exist
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu},\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)
such that
\gamma\left(t,\cdot\right)=X
,
\gamma\left(T,\cdot\right)=Y
, and

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)\leq\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
, since
\gamma(\cdot,\omega)\in\text{AC}([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\dot{\gamma}(s,\omega)
exists for almost every
s\in[t,T]
. Define
\alpha:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\alpha(s,\omega)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dot{\gamma}(s,\omega),\qquad\text{if the derivative exists},\\
&0,\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then
\alpha
is
\mathcal{B}([t,T])\otimes\mathcal{F}
-measurable (see, for example,
[
MM77
, Theorem 1]
). By Proposition
4.2
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\alpha_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq C,

for some constant
C=C(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0},T-t)>0
. Hence,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Now define
X_{s}(\omega):=\gamma(s,\omega)
for
s\in[t,T]
. Then
X_{s}
satisfies
X_{s}(\omega)=X(\omega)+\int_{t}^{s}\alpha(s,\omega)\,ds
and
\mathcal{L}(X_{T})=\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{T})=\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu
. Therefore,

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)\leq\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

Letting
\delta\to 0
yields
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq\tilde{U}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.
∎

In the definition of
\tilde{U}^{\varepsilon}_{1}
, the infimum over
\gamma
can be rewritten as the cost function
h
. This opens the door for applying the techniques developed in finite dimensional case
[
TY21
]
. Specifically, with assumptions (A1’), (A2) instead of (A1), (A2), we further reformulate
U_{1}^{\varepsilon}
in terms of the cost function
h^{\varepsilon}
in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume (A1’) and (A2), and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then

(4.11)

U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\mathbb{E}[h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X,Y)]+G(\nu)\right\},

where
\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}^{\ast}
is defined in (
4.5
), and
h^{\varepsilon}
is given in Definition
2.4
.

Note that the right-hand side of (
4.11
) is well defined since
h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,\cdot,\cdot)
is Borel measurable by Proposition
2.5
.

Proof.

Denote the right-hand side of (
4.11
) by

V^{\varepsilon}(t,m):=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\mathbb{E}[h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X,Y)]+G(\nu)\right\}.

By Proposition
4.4
, with assumptions (A1’) and (A2),

(4.12)

U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\left\{\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right)\,ds\right]\right\}+G(\nu)\right\}.

By the definition of
\Gamma(X,Y)
and
h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X,Y)
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)
and each fixed
\omega\in\Omega
,

\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s,\omega)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}_{s}(s,\omega)\right)\,ds\geq h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X(\omega),Y(\omega)).

Hence,
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)\geq V^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
. It remains to prove the reverse inequality.

If
t=T
, then any admissible pair
{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}
satisfies
X=Y
, which implies the only admissible measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
is
\nu=m
. Therefore,
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(T,m)=G(m)=V^{\varepsilon}(T,m)
.

Suppose
t<T
. Let
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
. Then there exist
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)\right]+G(\nu)\leq V^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\frac{\delta}{2}.

Since
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}
,
\mathbb{E}\left|X-Y\right|^{2}\leq M_{0}(T-t)^{2}
for some constant
M_{0}=M_{0}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
. Lemma
D.1
implies that there exists a Borel measurable path
\gamma:[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\gamma(\cdot,x,y)\in\mathrm{AC}([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\gamma(t,x,y)=x,\quad\gamma(T,x,y)=y,\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s,X,Y)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(s,X,Y)\right)\,ds\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)\right]+\frac{\delta}{2}.

Moreover,
(t,\omega)\mapsto\gamma(t,X(\omega),Y(\omega))
is
[t,T]\otimes\mathcal{F}
-measurable and admissible for
U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)
. Consequently,

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma(s,X,Y)}{\varepsilon},\dot{\gamma}(s,X,Y)\right)\,ds\right]+G\left(\nu\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)\right]+G\left(\nu\right)+\frac{\delta}{2}\leq V^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta.

Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, the conclusion follows.
∎

4.3.
Equivalence of
U^{\varepsilon},U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
, and
U^{\varepsilon}_{2}

We begin with a localization estimate that will be used repeatedly in the proof of
U^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{1}=U^{\varepsilon}_{2}
. The main point is that, along any admissible curve
\gamma
whose velocity is bounded in
L^{2}
in expectation, one may freeze the slow spatial variable and the measure argument on each subinterval of a uniform time partition at a total cost of order
O(N^{-1})
. More precisely, if
[t,T]
is divided into
N
subintervals and, on each
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
, the variables
x
and
m
in the Lagrangian are replaced by
\gamma_{t_{k}}
and
\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})
, respectively, then the total freezing error is bounded by
C(T-t)^{2}/N
.

The second part of the lemma identifies the infimum of the corresponding frozen cost. Since the frozen problem decouples across the subintervals and across sample points
\omega
, its value is given by the sum of the costs
h^{\varepsilon}
between the endpoints
\gamma_{t_{k}}
and
\gamma_{t_{k+1}}
. Moreover, one can choose measurable nearly minimizing curves on each subinterval such that the resulting concatenated admissible path still has bounded
L^{2}
velocity in expectation. This allows the freezing estimate to be applied not only to the original curve
\gamma
, but also to these piecewise nearly optimal paths.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\varepsilon>0,t\in[0,T),m,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}
, and
\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)
with

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M(T-t),

for some constant
M>0
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Delta t:=(T-t)/N
, and
t_{k}:=t+k\Delta t
for
k=0,\dots,N
. Then the following hold.

(1) There exists a constant
C=C(C_{1,H},C_{H},K_{0})>0
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\right|\,ds\right]\leq\frac{C(1+M)(T-t)^{2}}{N}.

(2) Define
\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma):=\{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y):\eta_{t_{k}}=\gamma_{t_{k}}\,\,\text{a.s. for all }k=0,\dots,N\}
. For
\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
, define

J_{\varepsilon}(\eta):=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\eta_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\eta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right].

Then

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

Moreover, for every
\delta\in(0,T-t)
, there exists a
\delta
-minimizer

\eta^{\delta}\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
of
J_{\varepsilon}
such that

\eta^{\delta}(s,\omega)=\eta^{\delta}_{k}\left(s,\gamma_{t_{k}}(\omega),\gamma_{t_{k+1}}(\omega)\right),\qquad s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],

where each
\eta^{\delta}_{k}:[t_{k},t_{k+1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Borel measurable and satisfies

\eta^{\delta}_{k}(\cdot,x,y)\in\mathrm{AC}([t_{k},t_{k+1}];\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\eta^{\delta}_{k}(t_{k},x,y)=x,\quad\eta^{\delta}_{k}(t_{k+1},x,y)=y,\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Furthermore, ther exists a constant
\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},M)>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq\tilde{M}(T-t),

and

(4.13)

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|L\left(\frac{\eta^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\eta^{\delta}_{s},\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\eta^{\delta}_{s})\right)-L\left(\frac{\eta^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\right|\,ds\right]\leq\frac{C(1+\tilde{M})(T-t)^{2}}{N}.

Proof.

We first prove (1). For each
k\in\left\{0,1,\cdots,N-1\right\}
, denote

E_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\right|\,ds\right].

For
s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
, by Hölder’s inequality,

{\bf d}_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\leq\left(\mathbb{E}|\gamma_{s}-\gamma_{t_{k}}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq(\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
A_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]
.
Since

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right]\leq(\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right)^{2}\leq(\Delta t)A_{k},

it follows from (
2.9
) that

\displaystyle E_{k}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left(1+2|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\right)\left(|\gamma_{s}-\gamma_{t_{k}}|+\mathbf{d}_{2}\bigl(\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\bigr)\right)\,ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(t_{k+1}-t_{k}+2\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right)\left(\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds+(\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}(\Delta t)\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right]\right)+2C_{L}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right]^{2}

\displaystyle+C_{L}(\Delta t)^{\frac{3}{2}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}+2C_{L}(\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|\,ds\right]\right)A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{L}(\Delta t)A_{k}+2C_{L}(\Delta t)^{\frac{3}{2}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using (
4.16
) and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}A_{k}\leq M(T-t),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sqrt{N}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}A_{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sqrt{N}\sqrt{M(T-t)}.

Hence,

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k}\leq\frac{4C_{L}M(T-t)^{2}}{N}+\frac{2C_{L}\sqrt{M}(T-t)^{2}}{N}.

We now prove (2). By the definition of
h^{\varepsilon}
, for any
\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
,

J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

Therefore,

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

To obtain the reverse inequality, let
\delta>0
.
By Lemma
D.1
, for each
k
and large
n
, there exists a Borel measurable path
\gamma_{n}^{k}:[t_{k},t_{k+1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\quad\gamma_{n}^{k}(\cdot,x,y)\in\mathrm{AC}([t_{k},t_{k+1};\mathbb{R}^{d}]),\quad\gamma_{n}^{k}(t_{k},x,y)=x,\quad\gamma_{n}^{k}(t_{k+1},x,y)=y,\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and

\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{n}^{k}(s,x,y)}{\varepsilon},x,\dot{\gamma}_{n}^{k}(s,x,y),\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds<h^{\varepsilon}\left(x,\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},x,y\right)+\frac{C_{N}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+|x-y|^{2}\right),

for some constant
C_{N}>0
. Since
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left|\gamma_{t_{k}}-\gamma_{t_{k_{1}}}\right|^{2}\leq\Delta t\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq\frac{M(T-t)^{2}}{N}
,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{n}^{k}(s,\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}})}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{n}^{k}(s,\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}),\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]+\frac{C_{N}}{\sqrt{n}},

for some constant
C_{N}>0
. Choose
n
sufficiently large so that
\frac{C_{N}}{\sqrt{n}}<\delta
and define

\eta^{\delta}(s,\omega):=\gamma^{k}_{n}\left(s,\gamma_{t_{k}}(\omega),\gamma_{t_{k+1}}(\omega)\right),\qquad s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],\,\,k=0,1,\cdots,N-1.

Then
\eta^{\delta}\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
and

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)\leq J_{\varepsilon}(\eta^{\delta})\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]+\delta.

This proves the reverse inequality and also gives the measurable representation of a
\delta
-minimizer.

Since
\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
is an admissible path,

(4.15)

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon}(\eta^{\delta})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y,\gamma)}J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)+\delta\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right]+(T-t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4C_{1,H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]+(K_{0}+1)(T-t)\leq\left(\frac{M}{4C_{1,H}}+K_{0}+1\right)(T-t),

where the third inequality follows from (
2.8
). On the other hand, again by (
2.8
),

J_{\varepsilon}(\eta^{\delta})\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{1}{4C_{2,H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]-K_{0}\Delta t\right)\geq\frac{1}{4C_{2,H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]-(T-t)K_{0}.

Combing with (
4.15
), we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq\tilde{M}(T-t),

where
\tilde{M}=4C_{2,H}\left(\frac{M}{4C_{1,H}}+2K_{0}+1\right)
.

Finally, applying the freezing estimate from part (1) to the path
\eta^{\delta}
, with
M
replaced by
\tilde{M}
, gives (
4.13
). The proof is complete.
∎

We are now ready to prove that
U^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{1}
. Although it is not clear whether, for an arbitrary
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, every coupling
\pi\in\Pi(m,\nu)
can be realized in the form
\mathcal{L}((X_{t},X_{T}))
, the admissible controls in
\mathcal{A}
generate “enough” couplings for the two value functions to coincide in the same sense that the Benamou–Brenier formulation of optimal transport covers all the “interesting” couplings.

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume (A1) and (A2). Then
U^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{1}=U^{\varepsilon}_{2}
.

Proof.

By Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
,

U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\left\{\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\right\}+G(\nu)\right\}.

Let
\delta>0
, and let
(\nu,X,Y,\gamma)
be
\delta
-optimal. Then by Proposition
4.2
,

(4.16)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t)

for some constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Delta t=(T-t)/N
,
t_{k}=t+k\Delta t
. By Lemma
4.6
,

(4.17)

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,m)+\delta

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right]-\frac{C}{N}+G(\nu)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]-\frac{C}{N}+G(\nu),

for some constant
C>0
independent of
N
.
By Lemma
4.6
again, for any
\delta>0
, there exists

\eta^{\delta}\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
such that

(4.18)

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\eta^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]+\delta,

and

(4.19)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\eta}_{s}^{\delta}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq\tilde{M}(T-t),

where
\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G})>0
is a constant. Moreover,

\eta^{\delta}(s,\omega)=\eta^{\delta}_{k}\left(s,\gamma_{t_{k}}(\omega),\gamma_{t_{k+1}}(\omega)\right),\qquad s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],

for some Borel measurable maps
\eta^{\delta}_{k}:[t_{k},t_{k+1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\eta^{\delta}_{k}(\cdot,x,y)\in\mathrm{AC}([t_{k},t_{k+1}];\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta^{\delta}_{k}(t_{k},x,y)=x,\;\eta^{\delta}_{k}(t_{k+1},x,y)=y
.

Since
\xi
is an independent Gaussian random variable, there exist Borel measurable functions
f_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
k=0,1,\cdots,N
such that

(4.20)

\left(\gamma_{t},\gamma_{t_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{T}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(f_{0}(\xi),f_{1}(\xi),\ldots,f_{N}(\xi)\right).

Define
\tilde{\alpha}^{\delta}:[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\tilde{\alpha}^{\delta}(s,z):=\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{k}(s,f_{k}(z),f_{k+1}(z)),\quad\text{ for }s\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],\quad k=0,1,\cdots,N-1,

and set
\alpha^{\delta}(s,\omega):=\tilde{\alpha}^{\delta}(s,\xi(\omega))
. Then (
4.19
) implies
\alpha^{\delta}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
. The corresponding controlled process satisfies

\displaystyle X_{s}^{\delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\eta^{\delta}(s,\cdot),\qquad\text{ for }s\in[t,T].

Hence, using (
4.17
)–(
4.19
) and Lemma
4.6
, we obtain

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,m)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{X_{s}^{\delta}}{\varepsilon},X_{s}^{\delta},\alpha_{s}^{\delta},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}^{\delta})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\eta^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\eta^{\delta}_{s},\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\eta^{\delta}_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\eta^{\delta}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\eta}^{\delta}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\,ds\right]+\frac{C}{N}+G(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]+\frac{C}{N}+\delta+G(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+2\delta+\frac{C}{N}.

Letting
\delta\to 0
, then
N\to\infty
, we conclude
U_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\leq U_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

We note that, the use of Lemma
4.6
in the above proof is to overcome the technical difficulty that appears only in the multiscale case. Specifically, without cutting the intervals, the coupling constructed in (
4.20
) needs to satisfy an infinite number of constaints, i.e.
\gamma_{t}=f_{t}(\xi)
for all
t
. The existence of such coupling is not clear to the best of our knowledge. When
H
does not have
x,m
dependence, such constraint no longer appears and the proof can be much simplified. In this simplified case, we do not need to freeze the slow variable and separate the time interval into
N
subintervals. The same proof as above can be directly conducted in
[t,T]
to prove
U^{\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{1}=U^{\varepsilon}_{2}
.

5.
Uniform convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
and rate of convergence under (A1’) and (A2)

By Corollary
3.2
, we know that
U^{\varepsilon}
satisfies

|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m_{1})-U^{\varepsilon}(s,m_{2})|\leq C_{0}\left(|t-s|+{\bf d}_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})\right),\;\;\text{for all }t,s\in[0,T],\;m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2},

where the constant
C_{0}
is independent of
\varepsilon
. Thus, the family
(U^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is uniformly equicontinuous and equibounded, hence by Arzela–Ascoli it converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
(up to subsequences) uniformly in the compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Moreover, since
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
is separable, we may obtain that
U^{\varepsilon}
converges pointwise to a function
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the same Lipschitz estimate as
U^{\varepsilon}
.

Indeed, let
\mathcal{D}
be a countable dense subset of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Then, a diagonal argument yields the pointwise convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
to a function
U\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is also Lipschitz with constant
C_{0}
over
\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is dense, we may extend
U
to a
C_{0}
-Lipschitz function (still denoted by
U
)
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. We argue that
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}U(t,m)
, for any
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
. For any
\eta>0
, we may choose
(t_{n},m_{n})\in\mathcal{D}
such that
|t-t_{n}|+{\bf d}_{2}(m,m_{n})\leq\eta/2C_{0}
. Then we write

\displaystyle\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U^{\varepsilon}(t_{n},m_{n})|+|U^{\varepsilon}(t_{n},m_{n})-U(t_{n},m_{n})|+|U(t_{n},m_{n})-U(t,m)|

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{0}(|t-t_{n}|+{\bf d}_{2}(m,m_{n}))+|U^{\varepsilon}(t_{n},m_{n})-U(t_{n},m_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq\eta+|U^{\varepsilon}(t_{n},m_{n})-U(t_{n},m_{n})|.

Sending
\varepsilon\to 0
, this implies
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq\eta
, and our claim follows by the arbitrariness of
\eta>0
.

In this section we strengthen the above convergence. Under assumptions (A1’) and (A2), we prove that
U^{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to a limit
\overline{U}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and we establish the optimal convergence rate
O(\varepsilon)
. The argument relies on the optimal control representation of
U^{\varepsilon}
. As shown in the previous section,
U^{\varepsilon}
admits an equivalent formulation in terms of the cost function
h^{\varepsilon}
. The subadditivity and superadditivity properties of
h
, summarized in Proposition
2.6
, then yield the optimal convergence rate.

5.1.
Uniform convergence and rate of convergence

Using the averaged metric
\overline{h}
, we define a new value function
\overline{U}
, which will be the uniform limit of
U^{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Define
\overline{U}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\overline{U}(t,m):=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)\right]+G(\nu)\right\},

where
\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}^{\ast}
is defined in (
4.5
), and
h^{\varepsilon}
is given in Proposition
2.6
.

We now present the uniform convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
to
\overline{U}
, together with the rate of convergence, in the following theorem. Moreover, the rate
O(\varepsilon)
is optimal; see Proposition
5.5
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume
(A1’)
and
(A2)
.
U^{\varepsilon}
converges to
\overline{U}
uniformly on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and there exists a constant
C=C\left(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G}\right)>0
such that for any
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

\left|U^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)-\overline{U}\left(t,m\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
. If
t=T
, by the definition of
m
in (
2.19
),

h^{\varepsilon}\left(t,t,x,y\right)=h^{\varepsilon}\left(0,0,x,y\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,\qquad\quad\text{ if }x=y,\\
&+\infty,\quad\,\text{ if }x\neq y.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Hence,

\overline{h}\left(t,t,x,y\right)=\overline{h}\left(0,0,x,y\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,\qquad\quad\text{ if }x=y,\\
&+\infty,\quad\,\text{ if }x\neq y,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

and

\overline{U}(t,m)=G(m)=U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
.

If
t<T
, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}
, by Proposition
2.6
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{h}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)-h^{\varepsilon}\left(t,T,X,Y\right)\right|\right]\leq C\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}|X-Y|^{2}}{(T-t)^{2}}\right)\varepsilon\leq C\left(1+M_{0}\right)\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
. Since
M_{0}=M_{0}\left(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0}\right)>0
,

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\mathbb{E}[h^{\varepsilon}(t,T,X,Y)]+G(\nu)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}(t,T,X,Y)]+G(\nu)\right\}+C(1+M_{0})\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\overline{U}(t,m)+\tilde{C}\varepsilon,

where
\tilde{C}=\tilde{C}(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},M_{0})=\tilde{C}(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G})>0
.
By a similar argument, we have

\overline{U}\left(t,m\right)\leq U^{\varepsilon}\left(t,m\right)+\tilde{C}\varepsilon.

∎

Remark 5.3
.

We do not identify
\overline{h}
in terms of
\overline{L}
at this stage; this will be addressed later (see Proposition
6.2
). Instead, in the next subsection, we employ the doubling-variable method to show that
\overline{U}
indeed solves the limiting equation. The reason for this approach is that identifying
\overline{h}
in terms of
\overline{L}
is highly nontrivial (see, for instance,
[
LPV87
,
Wei91
]
), and in more complicated settings such an identification may fail. By using the doubling-variable method, we make the structure of the argument transparent, allowing it to be extended to more general situations in future work.

5.2.
The limit
\overline{U}
and the limiting equation

To characterize the limit
\overline{U}
, we prove that it is the unique
L
-viscosity solution of (

HJB1

). For the proof, we will need the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

Assume that
H
satisfies Assumption (A1’). Suppose that
\delta>0
are fixed and for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
let
v^{\delta}(\cdot):=v^{\delta}(\cdot,p)
be the unique viscosity solution of (
2.10
).
For a fixed
P\in\mathcal{H}
, consider the function
V^{\delta}\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
with
V^{\delta}(X)=\mathbb{E}\left[v^{\delta}(X,P)\right]
and assume that for some fixed
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathcal{H})
, the function
V^{\delta}-\phi
admits a minimum at some
X_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
. Then,

(5.1)

\delta V^{\delta}(X_{0})+\mathbb{E}\left[H(X_{0},P+D\phi(X_{0}))\right]\geq 0

Proof.

The proof is divided in two parts.

Step 1.
(Alternative control formulation for
V^{\delta}
).

It is known that, for any
y\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
v^{\delta}
satisfies

v^{\delta}(y,p)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{det}}\left\{\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t})+\alpha_{t}\cdot p\right)dt\right\}\text{, where }\;\;X_{t}=y+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{s}ds

and
\mathcal{A}_{det}=\{\alpha:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\alpha\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([0,+\infty))\text{-measurable and }\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}|\alpha_{t}|^{2}dt<\infty\}.

In addition,
v^{\delta}(y,p)
is continuous and satisfies
|v^{\delta}(y,p)|\leq C(1+|p|^{2})
, for some constant
C>0
depending only on
\delta
and
H
. We let

V_{2}^{\delta}(X):=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t})+\alpha_{t}\cdot P\right)dt\right]\right\},

where
X_{t}=X+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{s}ds
and
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{A}(X,P)
is the set of controls

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(X,P)=\bigg\{\alpha\colon[0,+\infty)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\;\alpha\text{ is }\mathcal{B}([0,+\infty))\otimes

\displaystyle\;\sigma(X,P)\text{-measurable}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{t}|^{2}]dt<\infty\bigg\}.

We claim that for any
X\in\mathcal{H}

\mathbb{E}\left[v^{\delta}(X,P)\right]=V_{2}^{\delta}(X).

It is clear that
\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X,P)]\leq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)
, since
\alpha(\cdot,\omega)\in\mathcal{A}_{det}
for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(X,P)
. To prove the reverse inequality, we let
\varepsilon>0
and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we construct simple random variables
X^{n},P^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X^{n}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{n}}x_{k}{\bf 1}_{A_{k}}(\omega),\;\;\text{with }\;\|X^{n}-X\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n},\text{ and }

\displaystyle P^{n}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{n}}p_{k}{\bf 1}_{A_{k}}(\omega),\;\;\text{with }\;\|P^{n}-P\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n},

for some partition
(A_{k})_{k=1}^{N_{n}}
of the probability space
\Omega
. For any
k\in\{1,\ldots,N_{n}\}
, there exists an
\varepsilon
-optimal control
\alpha_{k}
for
v^{\delta}(x_{k},p_{k})
. We now consider the control

\alpha_{s}^{n}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{n}}(\alpha_{k})_{s}{\bf 1}_{A_{k}}(\omega).

and the processes
(X_{t}^{n})_{t\geq 0}
,
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with

X_{t}^{n}=X^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha^{n}_{s}ds\quad\text{and}\quad X_{t}=X+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha^{n}_{s}ds.

By the
\varepsilon
optimality and the quadratic growth of
L
we have for some constant
c,C

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[v^{\delta}(X^{n},P^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t}^{n},\alpha_{t}^{n})+\alpha_{t}^{n}\cdot P^{n}\right)dt\right]-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq c\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\|\alpha_{t}^{n}\|_{2}^{2}dt-C(1+\|P^{n}\|_{2}^{2}).

Thus, by the boundedness of
\delta v^{\delta}
and the fact that
\|P^{n}\|_{2}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|P\|_{2}
, we get the bound

(5.2)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\|\alpha_{t}^{n}\|_{2}^{2}dt\leq C_{\delta,P}+\varepsilon,

for some constant
C_{\delta,P}
depending only on
\delta,P
.

By the
\varepsilon
-optimality, once again, we have by the regularity properties of
L

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[v^{\delta}(X^{n},P^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t}^{n},\alpha_{t}^{n})+\alpha_{t}^{n}\cdot P^{n}\right)dt\right]-\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t}^{n})+\alpha_{t}^{n}\cdot P\right)dt\right]-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\left(L(X_{t}^{n},\alpha_{t}^{n})-L(X_{t},\alpha^{n}_{t})\right)dt\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\alpha_{t}^{n}\cdot\left(P^{n}-P\right)dt\right]

\displaystyle\geq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)-\varepsilon-\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}(1+|\alpha_{t}^{n}|)|X-X^{n}|dt\right]-\|P^{n}-P\|_{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}\|\alpha_{t}^{n}\|_{2}dt

\displaystyle\geq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)-\varepsilon-(\|X-X^{n}\|_{2}+\|P-P^{n}\|_{2})\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}(1+\|\alpha_{t}^{n}\|_{2})dt.

Since
v^{\delta}
is continuous and and satisfies
|v^{\delta}(y,p)|\leq C(1+|p|^{2})
, we have by the dominted convergence theorem
\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X^{n},P^{n})]\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X,P)]
, therefore, by choosing
n
large enough,

\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X,P)]+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\geq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)-\varepsilon-\frac{2}{n}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}(1+\|\alpha_{t}^{n}\|_{2})dt.

Combining this with (
5.2
) and setting
n
to be large enough, we get
\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X,P)]\geq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)-2\varepsilon
. The inequality
\mathbb{E}[v^{\delta}(X,P)]\geq V_{2}^{\delta}(X)
follows.

Step 2.
(Inequality (
5.1
) via dynamic programming)

We rely on the dynamic programming equality satisfied by
V_{2}^{\delta}
:

(5.3)

V_{2}^{\delta}(X)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{h}e^{-\delta t}(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t})+\alpha_{t}\cdot P)dt+e^{-\delta h}V_{2}^{\delta}(X_{h})\right],\text{ for all }h\geq 0\text{ and }X\in\mathcal{H},

where
X_{h}=X+\int_{0}^{h}\alpha_{s}ds
. Having (
5.3
) established, by classical arguments (for example see
[
FGS17
, Section 2.5]
), we get the viscosity supersolution inequality

\delta V^{\delta}_{2}(X_{0})+\sup_{a}\mathbb{E}[-L(X_{0},a)-a\cdot(D\phi(X_{0})+P))]\geq 0,

where the supremum is taken over all square integrable and
\sigma(X_{0},P)
-measurable random variables
a\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Inequality (
5.1
) then follows from the above because

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{-L(X_{0},a)-a\cdot(D\phi(X_{0})+P)\}]\geq\sup_{a}\mathbb{E}[-L(X_{0},a)-a\cdot(D\phi(X_{0})+P))].

We conclude the proof by showing (
5.3
). By the definition of
V_{2}^{\delta}
at the beginning of the proof, (
5.3
) hold as a
\geq
inequality. It suffices to prove the reverse. Let
\alpha^{1}\in\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(X,P)
and
X_{t}=X+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{s}^{1}ds
. For
\varepsilon>0
, we pick
\alpha^{2}\in\mathcal{A}(X_{h},P)
such that

\displaystyle V_{2}^{\delta}(X_{h})+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta t}(L(\widetilde{X}_{t},\alpha_{t}^{2})+\alpha^{2}_{t}\cdot P)dt\right]

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{h}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta(t-h)}(L(\widetilde{X}_{t-h},\alpha_{t-h}^{2})+\alpha^{2}_{t-h}\cdot P)dt\right],

where
\widetilde{X}_{t}=X_{h}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha^{2}_{s}ds,\;\;t\geq 0
. Consider the control
\widehat{\alpha}_{t}=\begin{cases}\alpha^{1}_{t},&t\in[0,h],\\
\alpha^{2}_{t-h},&t\in(h,+\infty).\end{cases}
We consider the process
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with
dY_{t}=\widehat{\alpha}_{t}dt,\;t\geq 0
and
Y_{0}=X
. We have
Y_{t}=X_{t},\;t\in[0,h]
and
Y_{t}=\widetilde{X}_{t-h},\;t\geq h
. Since
\sigma(X_{h},P)\subseteq\sigma(X,P)
, it is clear that
\widehat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}(X,P)
, therefore we have

\displaystyle V_{2}^{\delta}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{h}e^{-\delta t}(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t}^{1})+\alpha^{1}_{t}\cdot P)dt\right]+e^{-\delta h}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{h}^{+\infty}e^{-\delta(t-h)}(L(\widetilde{X}_{t-h},\alpha_{t-h}^{2})+\alpha^{2}_{t-h}\cdot P)dt\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{h}e^{-\delta t}(L(X_{t},\alpha_{t}^{1})+\alpha^{1}_{t}\cdot P)dt\right]+e^{-\delta h}\left(V_{2}^{\delta}(X_{h})+\varepsilon\right),

where in the last line we used (
5.4
). The result follows by sending
\varepsilon\to 0
and by minimizing over
\alpha^{1}\in\mathcal{A}(X,P)
.
∎

Proof of the viscosity property of the limit.

By Theorem
5.2
, we know that
U^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
converges to a function
U\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
uniformly. We will show that
U
satisfies (

HJB1

) in the viscosity sense. Then the result will follow by uniqueness of viscosity solutions provided by Theorem
3.8
.

We will only show the subsolution property, since the supersolution will be similar. We note that
U(T,m)=G(m)
, therefore we just need to verify the second property of Definition
3.6
. Let
\Phi\in C^{1,1}([0,T]\times\mathcal{H})
be a test function and assume that
\widehat{U}(t,X)-\Phi(t,X)
admits a strict global maximum with value zero at
(t_{0},X_{0})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{H}
. We assume that
t_{0}>0
, so there exists
r>0
such that
(t_{0}-r,t_{0}+r)\subset[0,T)
. The case
t_{0}=0
can be treated analogously by considering
r
such that
[0,r)\subset[0,T)
. Seeking a contradiction, we suppose that

(5.5)

-\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{H}(D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0}))\right]=\theta>0.

Our proof utilizes the idea of the perturbed test function method. We let
\delta>0
and, for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we consider
v^{\delta}(\cdot):=v^{\delta}(\cdot,p)
to be the solution of the discounted cell problem

(5.6)

\delta v^{\delta}+H(y,p+D_{y}v^{\delta})=0,\;\;y\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

We may consider the functions
V^{\delta}\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
with
V^{\delta}(X)=\mathbb{E}\left[v^{\delta}(X,D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})\right]
and
\Psi^{\varepsilon,\delta,\eta}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\Psi^{\varepsilon,\delta,\eta}(t,X,Y)=\Phi(t,X)+\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y)+\frac{\|Y-\frac{X}{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}+L\left(\|X-X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+|t-t_{0}|^{2}\right),

Consider
r>0
small enough (to be chosen later) and the set

A_{r}=(t_{0}-r,t_{0}+r)\times\left\{X\in\mathcal{H}:\;\|X-X_{0}\|_{2}<r\right\}.

By the perturbed optimization result of Ekeland-Lebourg
[
FGS17
, Theorem 3.25]
for every
\mathbb{N}\ni n\geq 1
, there exist
a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
X_{n},Y_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
|a_{n}|+\|X_{n}\|_{2}+\|Y_{n}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}
and the function

[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\ni(t,X,Y)\mapsto\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,X)-\Psi^{\varepsilon,\delta,\eta}(t,X,Y)-a_{n}t-\mathbb{E}[X_{n}\cdot X]-\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\cdot Y]-\varepsilon^{3}\|Y\|_{2}^{2}

attains a maximum at some point
(\overline{t},\overline{X},\overline{Y})
such that
(\overline{t},\overline{X})\in\overline{A}_{r}
, where
\overline{A}_{r}
is the closure of the set
A_{r}
defined above, and
\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}<\varepsilon^{-3/2}
.

By comparing the values of the above function at
(\overline{t},\overline{X},\overline{Y})
and at
(\overline{t},\overline{X},\overline{X}/\varepsilon)
, we obtain

(5.7)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(\overline{X}/\varepsilon)-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Y_{n}\cdot\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}\right]-\varepsilon^{3}\left\|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})-\frac{\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}/\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\cdot\overline{Y}]-\varepsilon^{3}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}.

Consequently, for
n
large enough such that
\frac{1}{n}\leq\varepsilon^{5}
, we discover due to the uniform Lipschitz bound for
v^{\delta}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{3}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}/\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\left(V^{\delta}(\overline{X}/\varepsilon)-V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})\right)+\varepsilon\|\overline{X}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\cdot\overline{Y}]+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\cdot\overline{X}]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|Dv^{\delta}\|_{\infty}\left\|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right\|_{2}+2\varepsilon(r^{2}+\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2})+\varepsilon^{5}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|\overline{X}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right\|_{2}+\varepsilon^{5}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}+4\varepsilon(r^{2}+\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(r^{2}+1)+\frac{\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\eta}+\frac{C^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{3}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{7}}{2},

which gives

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}/\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}\leq\eta\varepsilon C(r^{2}+1)\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon^{2}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C(r^{2}+1),

for some constant
C>0
depending only on the data and
X_{0}
. Going back to (
5.7
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}/\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\left(V^{\delta}(\overline{X}/\varepsilon)-V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})\right)-\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}(\cdot\overline{Y}-\overline{X}/\varepsilon)]+\varepsilon^{3}\left(\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}-\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}+\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\|\overline{X}\|_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}\right),

which, due to (
5.8
), yields

(5.9)

\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}\leq C\varepsilon\eta,

for some constant
C>0
depending only on the data and
X_{0}
.

We consider 2 cases depending on whether
(\overline{t},\overline{X})
is on the boundary or in the interior of
\overline{A}_{r}
.

Case 1.
We start with the case where
(\overline{t},\overline{X})
is in the interior. Now, by the definition of viscosity solutions for
\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}
we have

(5.10)

\begin{split}-\partial_{t}\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-a_{n}-2L(t&-t_{0})\\
&+\mathbb{E}\left[H\left(\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon},\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y})}{\varepsilon\eta}+2L(\overline{X}-X_{0})+X_{n}+D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})\right)\right]\leq 0.\end{split}

In addition, since the function

\mathcal{H}\ni Y\mapsto\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y)+\frac{\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-Y\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}+\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\cdot Y]+\varepsilon^{3}\|Y\|_{2}^{2}

admits a minimum at
\overline{Y}
, Proposition
5.4
yields

(5.11)

\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})+\mathbb{E}\left[H\left(\overline{Y},D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})+\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y})}{\varepsilon\eta}-2\overline{Y}\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\geq 0.

Due to the regularity of
\Phi
, we may choose
r
small enough such that

(5.12)

\left|\partial_{t}\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})\right|+\left\|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})\right\|_{2}\leq\delta^{2}/3.

We add (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), and we get by the properties of
H

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq-\partial_{t}\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})-a_{n}-2L(t-t_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathbb{E}\left[H\left(\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon},\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y})}{\varepsilon\eta}+2L(\overline{X}-X_{0})+X_{n}+D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\qquad-\mathbb{E}\left[H\left(\overline{Y},D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})+\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y})}{\varepsilon\eta}-2\overline{Y}\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq-\partial_{t}\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})-a_{n}-2L(t-t_{0})

\displaystyle\;-C_{H}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(1+2Lr+\frac{1}{n}+2\varepsilon^{2}\left|\overline{Y}\right|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|+|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})|+|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})|+\left|\frac{4(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y})}{\varepsilon\eta}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 14.22636pt\cdot\left(\left|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right|+|2L(\overline{X}-X_{0})|+|X_{n}|+|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})-D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})|+2\varepsilon^{2}|\overline{Y}|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|\right)\bigg]

We now use Cauchy-Schwarz, (
5.12
) and the fact that
\frac{1}{n}\leq\varepsilon^{5}
to obtain

\displaystyle 0\geq-\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-\frac{\delta^{2}}{3}-\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})-\frac{1}{n}-2Lr

\displaystyle\;\;-C_{H}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(1+2Lr+\varepsilon^{5}+2\varepsilon^{2}|\overline{Y}|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|+|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})|+|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})|+\left|\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}}{\varepsilon\eta}\right|\right)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\left|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right|+2Lr+|X_{n}|+|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})|+2\varepsilon^{2}|\overline{Y}|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|\right)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}.

However, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[

\displaystyle\left(1+2Lr+\varepsilon^{5}+2\varepsilon^{2}|\overline{Y}|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|+|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})|+|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})|+\left|\frac{2(\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}}{\varepsilon\eta}\right|\right)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq 1+2Lr+\varepsilon^{5}+2\varepsilon^{2}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}+\|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})\|_{2}+\|D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})\|_{2}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon\eta}\|\overline{X}/\varepsilon-\overline{Y}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq 1+(2Lr)^{2}+\varepsilon^{5}+\varepsilon C\sqrt{r^{2}+1}+\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{3}+2\|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})\|_{2}+2C=:M,

where we used (
5.12
), (
5.9
) and (
5.8
), and
M
is bounded when
\varepsilon,r,\delta
are small. In addition,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[

\displaystyle\left(\left|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right|+2Lr+|X_{n}|+|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})|+2\varepsilon^{2}|\overline{Y}|+\left|\frac{Y_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right|\right)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left\|\frac{\overline{X}}{\varepsilon}-\overline{Y}\right\|_{2}+2Lr+\|X_{n}\|_{2}+\|D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-D\Phi(\overline{t},\overline{X})\|_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|Y_{n}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C\eta\varepsilon+2Lr+\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{3}+\varepsilon C+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}\leq C\varepsilon+2Lr+\frac{\delta^{2}}{3}.

Using the last two inequalities in (
5.13
) when
Lr,\varepsilon,\eta
and
\delta
are chosen to be small enough we get

\frac{\theta}{2}\geq-\partial_{t}\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y}).

However, by Proposition
2.2
, for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
-\delta v^{\delta}(\cdot,p)
converges uniformly to
\overline{H}(p)
as
\delta\to 0
, hence
-\delta V^{\delta}(\overline{Y})
converges to
\mathbb{E}[\overline{H}(D\Phi(t_{0},X_{0}))]
as
\delta\to 0
.
This contradicts (
5.5
) if
\delta
is small enough, so we may proceed with case 2.

Case 2.
We now assume that
(\overline{t},\overline{X})
is on the boundary of
\overline{A}_{r}
. Note, also, that it follows from (
5.8
) that
\|\overline{Y}\|_{2}<\varepsilon^{-3/2}
. We will get a contradiction by proving that

(5.14)

\begin{split}\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}(t,X)-\Phi(t,X)-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y)&-\frac{\|X/\varepsilon-Y\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}-L\left(\|X-X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+|t-t_{0}|^{2}\right)\\
&\quad-\varepsilon^{3}\|Y\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[X\cdot X_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[Y\cdot Y_{n}]+a_{n}t<-\frac{Lr^{2}}{2},\end{split}

for any
(t,X)
on the boundary of
\overline{A}_{r}
, any
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{2}<\varepsilon^{-3/2}
, any
\varepsilon
small enough and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\frac{1}{n}<\varepsilon^{5}
. Indeed, if (
5.14
) is true, then since
(\overline{t},\overline{X},\overline{Y})
is a position of maximum, we also have

\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0},X_{0})-\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})-\varepsilon V^{\delta}\left(\frac{X_{0}}{\varepsilon}\right)-\varepsilon\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{0}\cdot X_{n}+\frac{X_{0}}{\varepsilon}\cdot Y_{n}\right]+a_{n}t<-\frac{Lr^{2}}{2}.

By sending
n\to+\infty
and then
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get
0=\widehat{U}(t_{0},X_{0})-\Phi(t_{0},X_{0})<-\frac{Lr^{2}}{2}
, which is a contradiction.

To show (
5.14
), we again argue by contradiction. Suppose that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})
on the boundary of
\overline{A}_{r}
and
Y^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{H}
with
\|Y^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}<\varepsilon^{-3/2}
such that

\begin{split}\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})-\Phi(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y^{\varepsilon})&-\frac{\|X^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon-Y^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}-L\left(\|X^{\varepsilon}-X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+|t^{\varepsilon}-t_{0}|^{2}\right)\\
&\quad-\varepsilon^{3}\|Y^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[X^{\varepsilon}\cdot X_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[Y^{\varepsilon}\cdot Y_{n}]+a_{n}t^{\varepsilon}\geq-\frac{Lr^{2}}{2},\end{split}

for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\frac{1}{n}<\varepsilon^{5}
. Since
(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})
is on the boundary of
\overline{A}_{r}
, we have the inequality
L\left(\|X^{\varepsilon}-X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+|t^{\varepsilon}-t_{0}|^{2}\right)\geq Lr^{2}
. In addition,
\widehat{U}(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})\leq\Phi(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})
, so we get from the above

\widehat{U}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})-\widehat{U}(t^{\varepsilon},X^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y^{\varepsilon})-\mathbb{E}[X^{\varepsilon}\cdot X_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[Y^{\varepsilon}\cdot Y_{n}]+a_{n}t^{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{Lr^{2}}{2},

or

\sup_{(t,X)\in\overline{A}_{r}}|U^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathcal{L}(X))-U(t,\mathcal{L}(X))|-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y^{\varepsilon})+\|X^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}\|X_{n}\|_{2}+\|Y^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}\|Y_{n}\|_{2}+|a_{n}||t^{\varepsilon}|\geq\frac{Lr^{2}}{2},

or

\sup_{(t,X)\in\overline{A}_{r}}|U^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathcal{L}(X))-U(t,\mathcal{L}(X))|-\varepsilon V^{\delta}(Y^{\varepsilon})+\|X^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}\frac{1}{n}+\|Y^{\varepsilon}\|_{2}\frac{1}{n}+|t^{\varepsilon}|\frac{1}{n}\geq\frac{Lr^{2}}{2}.

We now send
\varepsilon\to 0
and
n\to\infty
to get a contradiction due to the uniform convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
to
U
, the boundedness of
V^{\delta}
and the uniform boundedness of
(\|X^{\varepsilon}\|_{2})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
(t^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
and optimality of the rate
O(\varepsilon)
.

We collect the results of the previous subsections to prove Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Combining the result of Theorem
5.2
and the proof presented in subsection
5.2
, the family
(U^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
converges uniformly and with rate
O(\varepsilon)
(equation (
1.11
)) to an
L
-viscosity solution
U
of (

HJB1

). By Theorem
3.8
, (

HJB1

) has a unique
L
-viscosity solution. This fully characterizes the limit
U
. The optimality of the rate
O(\varepsilon)
follows from Proposition
5.5
below.
∎

We finally present an example in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
adapted from
[
MTY19
, Proposition 4.3]
, which shows the optimality of
O(\varepsilon)
rate in Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
H(x,p)=\frac{|p|^{2}}{2}+V(x)
, where
V\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfies
\max_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}V(x)=0
and
V(x)\leq-1
for all
x\in B\left(0,\frac{1}{3}\right)
. Suppose
G(m)\equiv 0
for all
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. For
\varepsilon>0
, let
U^{\varepsilon}
be the solution to (

HJB
ε

) associated with this Hamiltonian
H
, and let
U
be the solution to (

HJB1

) with the effective Hamiltonian
\overline{H}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
corresponding to
H
.

Then
U^{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
U\equiv 0
on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and

U^{\varepsilon}(T-1,\delta_{0})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Proof.

Since
\overline{H}(0)=0
(see
[
Tra21
, Lemma 4.21]
), the function
U\equiv 0
solves (

HJB1

). Indeed, the lifted function
\widehat{U}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
\widehat{U}(t,X)\equiv 0
satisfies

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}\widehat{U}(t,X)+\widehat{\overline{H}}(D\widehat{U}(t,X))=0,&(t,X)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{H},\\
\widehat{U}(T,X)=0,&X\in\mathcal{H},\end{cases}

where
\widehat{\overline{H}}(P)=\mathbb{E}[\overline{H}(P)]\;\;\text{for }P\in\mathcal{H}
. By the value function representation of
U^{\varepsilon}
,

U^{\varepsilon}(T-1,\delta_{0})=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{|\alpha_{s}|^{2}}{2}-V\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\,ds\right],\qquad dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}\,ds,\ \ X_{0}=0.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and let
X
be the associated controlled process. Define

S=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:X_{s}(\omega)\in B\left(0,\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)\text{ for all }s\in\left[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right]\right\}.

If
\omega\in S
, then
V(X_{s}/\varepsilon)\leq-1
on
[0,\varepsilon/3]
, hence

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\left|\alpha_{s}\right|^{2}}{2}-V\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon}\right)\,ds\geq\int_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}-V\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon}\right)\,ds\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

If
\omega\in S^{c}
, let
t_{0}(\omega)\leq\varepsilon/3
be the first exit time from
B(0,\varepsilon/3)
. Then
|X_{t_{0}}|=\varepsilon/3
and

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\left|\alpha_{s}\right|^{2}}{2}-V\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon}\right)\,ds\geq\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{\left|\alpha_{s}\right|^{2}}{2}\,ds\geq\frac{1}{2t_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\alpha_{s}\,ds\right)^{2}=\frac{\left|X_{t_{0}}\right|^{2}}{2t_{0}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\left|\alpha_{s}\right|^{2}}{2}-V\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon}\right)\,ds\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\mathbb{P}\left(S\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\mathbb{P}\left(S^{c}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Taking the infimum over
\alpha
completes the proof.
∎

6.
Extension to Macroscopic and Measure-Dependent Lagrangians

In this section, we assume (A1) and (A2). The Hamiltonian is now allowed to depend on the slow variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and probability measure
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, and the constants appearing in (A1’) are uniform with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. We extend the result from the previous case, obtained under assumptions (A1’) and (A2), to this more general setting. The rate of convergence in this case is
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.

6.1.
Equivalent formulations of
U

As in the case of
U^{\varepsilon}
, by the dynamic programming principle
[
DPT22b
]
, the solution
U
to the limiting equation (

HJB2

) admits a variational representation. In particular,
U
satisfies (
1.13
). We also provide an alternative formulation of
U
and a velocity bound for nearly optimal paths. The proof is omitted as it follows the same argument as in Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
.

Proposition 6.1
.

(1) For any
t\in[0,T]
and
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,

\displaystyle U(t,m)=

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}\overline{L}\left(X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\mathcal{L}(X_{T}))\right\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left\{\inf_{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{m,\nu}}\left\{\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\right\}+G(\nu)\right\}.

Here,
\mathcal{A}
is the control set defined in (
1.2
), and
X_{s}
solves
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}\,ds
.
\mathcal{E}_{m,\nu}^{\ast}
is defined in (
4.5
), and
\Gamma(X,Y)
is defined in (
4.2
).

(2) Let
t\in[0,T)
and
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
. Suppose
(\nu_{\delta},X^{\delta},Y^{\delta},\gamma^{\delta})
is a
\delta
-minimizer for
U(t,m)
.
Then there exists a constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}^{\delta}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t).

6.2.
Convergence of the cost function
h^{\varepsilon}

The rate of convergence of
h^{\varepsilon}
is summarized below as a direct consequence of Proposition
2.6
. In particular, the constant
C
in the
O(\varepsilon)
convergence rate is independent of
c,\mu
. Moreover, the limit can be characterized in terms of the effective Lagrangian
\overline{L}
, as established in the existing literature (see, for example,
[
LPV87
,
Wei91
]
).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0,\ 0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T,x,y,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Then the limit

\overline{h}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon h\left(c,\mu;\frac{t_{1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{t_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}\right)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}h^{\varepsilon}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)

exists. Moreover, there exists a constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
independent of
c,\mu,t_{1},t_{2},x,y
such that

\displaystyle\left|\overline{h}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)-h^{\varepsilon}\left(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y\right)\right|\leq C\left(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}\right)\varepsilon.

Finally, the limit admits the representation

\displaystyle\overline{h}(c,\mu;t_{1},t_{2},x,y)

\displaystyle=(t_{2}-t_{1})\,\overline{L}\!\left(c,\frac{y-x}{t_{2}-t_{1}},\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\overline{L}(c,\dot{\gamma}(s),\mu)\,ds:\gamma\in\mathrm{AC}\left([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathbb{R}^{d}\right),\gamma(t_{1})=x,\gamma(t_{2})=y\right\}.

6.3.
Rate of convergence of
U^{\varepsilon}
to
U

Before proving the convergence rate, we establish the following lemma, which is analogous to Lemma
4.6
and shows that the same decomposition property holds for
\overline{L}
: the minimal action over a fixed time partition can be written as a sum of local minimal costs on each subinterval.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0,t\in[0,T),\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mu_{0},\mu}^{\ast}
, and
\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)
satisfy

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t),

with
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
from Proposition
4.2
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Delta t:=(T-t)/N
, and
t_{k}:=t+k\Delta t
for
k=0,\dots,N
. Define
\Gamma(X,Y,\gamma)
as in Lemma
4.6
, that is,
\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma):=\{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y):\eta_{t_{k}}=\gamma_{t_{k}}\,\,\text{a.s. for all }k=0,\dots,N\}
. For
\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
, define

\overline{J}(\eta):=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\eta}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}}\right)\right)\,dt\right]

Then

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}\overline{J}(\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}}\right);t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

The infimum is attained by the piecewise linear interpolation

(6.1)

\overline{\gamma}(s,\omega):=\gamma(t_{k},\omega)+\frac{s-t_{k}}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}\left(\gamma(t_{k+1},\omega)-\gamma(t_{k},\omega)\right),\quad\text{ for }\omega\in\Omega,\,\,s\in\left[t_{k},t_{k+1}\right],\,\,k=0,\dots,N-1,

and
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t)
.

Proof.

By definition,

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}\overline{J}(\eta)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

By Proposition
6.2
,

\overline{h}\bigl(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\bigr)=\left(t_{k+1}-t_{k}\right)\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\frac{\gamma_{t_{k+1}}-\gamma_{t_{k}}}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right).

Consider the path
\overline{\gamma}:[t,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined in (
6.1
). Then
\overline{\gamma}\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
, and

\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y,\gamma)}J(\eta)\leq\overline{J}(\overline{\gamma})=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

Hence,
\overline{\gamma}
attains the infimum.
We compute

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]=\frac{1}{\Delta t}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left|\gamma_{t_{k+1}}-\gamma_{t_{k}}\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t).

∎

We are now ready to prove the convergence rate in the general setting under assumptions (A1) and (A2).

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

If
t_{0}=T
, by Propositions
4.2
,
4.4
,
4.7
and
6.1
, the admissible pair
(X,Y)
must satisfy
X=Y
, and the only admissible measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
is
\nu=m
. It follows that
U^{\varepsilon}(T,m)=G(m)=U(T,m)
.

Assume from now on that
t<T
and let
\delta\in\left(0,T-t\right)
.

Step 1: Lower bound for
U^{\varepsilon}-U
.
By Propositions
4.2
,
4.4
, and
4.7
, let
(\nu,X,Y,\gamma)
be
\delta
-optimal for
U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)
. Then

(6.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)\leq U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}|\dot{\gamma}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t),

for some constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.

Consider a time partition
t_{k}:=t+\frac{k}{N}(T-t)
for
k=0,\dots,N
, where
N\in\mathbb{N}
will be chosen later. With
J_{\varepsilon}
as in Lemma
4.6
, we may write

(6.3)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\geq J_{\varepsilon}(\gamma)-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k},

where

E_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)-L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\right|\,ds\right].

By Lemma
4.6
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon}(\gamma)\geq\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}J_{\varepsilon}(\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

Next, Proposition
6.2
implies that

(6.5)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]\\
&\qquad\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]-C\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|\gamma_{k+1}-\gamma_{k}\right|^{2}}{|t_{k+1}-t_{k}|^{2}}\right)\varepsilon,\end{split}

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
.
By Hölder’s inequality,

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|\gamma_{k+1}-\gamma_{k}\right|^{2}}{|t_{k+1}-t_{k}|^{2}}\leq\frac{N}{T-t}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\right]\,ds\leq M_{1}N,

where we use the fact that
t_{k+1}-t_{k}=\frac{T-t_{0}}{N}
and (
6.2
). Combining this with (
6.5
) yields

(6.6)

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]-CN\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.

Let
\overline{\gamma}:[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the piecewise linear interpolation of
\gamma
on the partition
\{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
, namely

\overline{\gamma}(t,\omega):=\gamma(t_{k},\omega)+\frac{s-t_{k}}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}\left(\gamma(t_{k+1},\omega)-\gamma(t_{k},\omega)\right),\qquad\text{ for }t\in\left[t_{k},t_{k+1}\right].

By Lemma
6.3
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]=\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}\overline{J}(\eta)=\overline{J}(\overline{\gamma})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\overline{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\,ds\right]-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k},

where

\overline{E}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)-\overline{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\right|\,ds\right].

Combining (
6.3
), (
6.4
), (
6.6
), and (
6.7
), we obtain

(6.8)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\gamma_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\overline{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\,ds\right]-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k}-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k}-CN\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.
By Lemmas
4.6
,
6.3
, and (
6.2
),

(6.9)

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k}\leq\frac{C(1+M_{1})(T-t)^{2}}{N},\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k}\leq\frac{C(1+M_{1})(T-t)^{2}}{N}.

Combining (
6.2
), (
6.8
), and (
6.9
), we deduce that

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)+\delta

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\overline{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\overline{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\overline{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)-\frac{C(T-t)^{2}}{N}-CN\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq U(t,m)-\frac{C(T-t)^{2}}{N}-CN\varepsilon.

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.
Letting
\delta\to 0
, we obtain

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\geq U(t,m)-\frac{C(T-t)^{2}}{N}-CN\varepsilon.

If
T-t\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, choose
N=1
. Then

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\geq U(t,m)-C(T-t)^{2}-C\varepsilon\geq U(t,m)-C\varepsilon.

If
T-t>\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, choose
N=\left\lfloor\frac{T-t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rfloor+1
. Then

\frac{(T-t)^{2}}{N}\leq(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},\qquad\qquad N\varepsilon\leq 2(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},

and hence

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)\geq U(t,m)-C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Therefore,

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\geq\begin{cases}-C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},&T-t\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\[4.0pt]
-C\varepsilon,&0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Step 2: Upper bound for
U^{\varepsilon}-U
.
By Lemma
6.1
, there exist
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2},(X,Y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}_{\mu_{0},\mu}
, and
\gamma\in\Gamma(X,Y)
satisfying

(6.10)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]+G(\nu)\leq U(t,m)+\delta,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\gamma}_{s}\right|^{2}\,ds\right]\leq M_{1}(T-t),

for some constant
M_{1}=M_{1}(C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.

Consider the same time partition
t_{k}:=t+\frac{k}{N}(T-t)
for
k=0,\dots,N
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
to be determined. Then

(6.11)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\geq\overline{J}(\gamma)-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k},

where

\overline{E}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma_{t}\right)\right)-\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)\right|\,ds\right].

By Lemma
6.3
,

(6.12)

\overline{J}(\gamma)\geq\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)}\overline{J}(\eta)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right].

Using Proposition
6.2
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]\\
&\qquad\geq\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]-C\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|\gamma_{k+1}-\gamma_{k}\right|^{2}}{|t_{k+1}-t_{k}|^{2}}\right)\varepsilon,\end{split}

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0})>0
. Using the velocity bound (
6.10
) exactly as in Step 1 yields

(6.13)

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{h}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]-CN\varepsilon,

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.

By Lemma
4.6
, there exists
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma(X,Y;\gamma)
such that

(6.14)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left|\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{s}\right|^{2}dt\right]\leq\tilde{M}(T-t),

and

(6.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{t_{k}},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}});t_{k},t_{k+1},\gamma_{t_{k}},\gamma_{t_{k+1}}\right)\right]+\delta\geq J_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\gamma})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}L\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\tilde{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\,ds\right]-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k},

where

E_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left|L\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\gamma_{t_{k}},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t_{k}})\right)-L\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\tilde{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\right|\,ds\right],

and
\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},M_{1})=\tilde{M}(C_{1,H},C_{2,H},K_{0},C_{G})>0
is some constant.

Combining (
6.11
), (
6.12
), (
6.13
), and (
6.15
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{t}\overline{L}\left(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s})\right)\,ds\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{s}}{\varepsilon},\tilde{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{s},\mathcal{L}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{s}\right)\right)\,ds\right]-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k}-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k}-CN\varepsilon-\delta.

Using the velocity bounds (
6.10
) and (
6.14
) as in Step 1,

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}E_{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\overline{E}_{k}\leq\frac{C(T-t)^{2}}{N},

and hence

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\leq\frac{C(T-t)^{2}}{N}+CN\varepsilon

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},K_{0})>0
.

Choosing
N
exactly as in Step 1, we conclude that

U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\leq\begin{cases}C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},&T-t\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\[4.0pt]
C\varepsilon,&0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Consequently,

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq\begin{cases}C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},&T-t\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\[4.0pt]
C\varepsilon,&0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Finally, by Corollary
3.2
, we have

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-G(m)\right|=\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U^{\varepsilon}(T,m)\right|\leq C_{0}(T-t),

and similarly

\left|U(t,m)-G(m)\right|=\left|U(t,m)-U(T,m)\right|\leq C_{0}(T-t),

for some constant
C_{0}>0
. Therefore,

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq 2C_{0}(T-t).

In particular, if
0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq C\min\{T-t,\varepsilon\}

for some constant
C=C(d,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{G},C_{H},K_{0})>0
, and

\left|U^{\varepsilon}(t,m)-U(t,m)\right|\leq\begin{cases}C(T-t)\sqrt{\varepsilon},&T-t\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\[4.0pt]
C\min\{T-t,\varepsilon\},&0\leq T-t<\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

∎

7.
Dynamic Optimal Transport Problem

In this section, we prove our results for the dynamic optimal transport problem
U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}
. Recall that for
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
t\in[0,T)
,
U^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)
is the value function

U_{ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\;:=\;\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}}\;\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}L\Big(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},X_{s},\alpha_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s})\Big)\,ds\Bigg],

where
(X_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
satisfies
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds
,
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu
and
\mathcal{L}(X_{T})=\nu
and
\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}
was defined in (
1.5
). As in the previous sections, we consider the control sets

\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{1}:=\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{1}:\;\exists X_{t}\text{ such that }\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu\text{ and }\mathcal{L}(X_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\alpha_{s}ds)=\nu\right\}

and

\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{2}:=\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{2}:\;\exists X_{t}\text{ such that }\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mu\text{ and }\mathcal{L}(X_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\alpha_{s}ds)=\nu\right\}

where the sets
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
were introduced in Section
4.1
. The corresponding value functions are denoted by
U_{1,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)
and
U_{2,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)
, respectively. Since
\mathcal{A}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}
, it is clear that

(7.1)

U_{1,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\leq U_{ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\leq U_{2,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu),\;\;\text{for any }(t,\mu,\nu)\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}.

We observe that
\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{1}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{2}\neq\emptyset
, since the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is assumed to be rich enough. Indeed, we may pick
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable
X,Y\in L^{2}
with
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu
and
\mathcal{L}(Y)=\nu
and
X_{s}=\frac{s-t}{T-t}Y+\frac{T-s}{T-t}X
is an admissible state process.

The following lemma is a consequence of the quadratic growth of
L
(equation (
2.8
)).

Lemma 7.1
.

Fix
t\in[0,T)
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Then,

\frac{1}{2C_{2,H}(T-t)}{\bf d}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)-K_{0}(T-t)\leq U_{1,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\leq U_{2,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)\leq\frac{1}{2C_{1,H}(T-t)}{\bf d}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)+K_{0}(T-t).

In order to prove Theorem
1.3
, our first step will be to show that
U^{\varepsilon}_{1,ot}=U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}
. Then we proceed with a similar proof strategy as the one we used for Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
(see Section
6
for the proof of the latter). Recalling the equality
U^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu)=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\{U^{\varepsilon}_{ot}(t,\mu,\nu)+G(\nu)\}
, the proof of the first step relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be two lower bounded functions such that

(7.2)

\inf_{m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\{f_{1}(m)+G(m)\right\}=\inf_{m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\{f_{2}(m)+G(m)\right\},

for any bounded and
{\bf d}_{2}
-Lipschitz function
G\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
. If
f_{1}
is assumed to be lower semi-continuous, then
f_{1}(m)\leq f_{2}(m)
, for all
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, if both
f_{1},f_{2}
are lower semi-continuous, then
f_{1}(m)=f_{2}(m)
, for all
m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction. Assume that
f_{1}(m_{0})>f_{2}(m_{0})
, for some
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
G_{N}(m)=N\min\{1,{\bf d}_{2}(m,m_{0})\}
, which is bounded and
{\bf d}_{2}
-Lipschitz with
G_{N}(m_{0})=0
and
G_{N}\geq 0
. Then, (
7.2
) implies

\inf_{m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}\{f_{1}(m)+G_{N}(m)\}\leq f_{2}(m_{0})+G_{N}(m_{0})=f_{2}(m_{0}).

Let
\delta>0
such that
\delta<f_{1}(m_{0})-f_{2}(m_{0})
. Then, there exists
m^{\delta,N}
such that

(7.3)

f_{1}(m^{\delta,N})+G_{N}(m^{\delta,N})-\delta\leq f_{2}(m_{0}).

Since
f_{1}
is lower bounded, this inequality gives a uniform in
N
upper bound for
G_{N}(m^{\delta,N})
, which in turn implies
m^{\delta,N}\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}m_{0}
in
\textbf{d}_{2}
metric. Sending
N\to\infty
in (
7.3
) we get, by the semi-continuity of
f_{1}
and the non-negativity of
G_{N}
,

f_{1}(m_{0})-\delta\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}f_{1}(m^{\delta,N})-\delta\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}(f_{1}(m^{\delta,N})+G_{N}(m^{\delta,N}))-\delta\leq f_{2}(m_{0}).

This contradicts the choice of
\delta
.

If
f_{2}
is also lower semi-continuous, then
f_{2}\leq f_{1}
. Therefore, combining both inequalities,
f_{1}=f_{2}
.
∎

7.1.
Continuity properties of the value function

We prove that
U^{\varepsilon}_{1,ot},U^{\varepsilon}_{2,ot}\colon[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous functions. To lighten the notation in the proofs, for this subsection only, we assume that
H
satisfies Assumption (A1’). The case where
H
satisfies (A1) can be treated by adapting the arguments that follow.

For
i=1,2
, we consider the sets

\mathcal{A}^{i,\delta}_{t,\mu,\nu}=\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{i}:\;\alpha_{s}=0,\;s\in[t,t+\delta]\right\},\;\;\text{for }\delta\in(0,T-t).

We first show that the value function of the optimization problems
U^{\varepsilon}_{i,ot},\;i=1,2
remains the same if we restrict to controls in
\mathcal{A}_{t,\mu,\nu}^{i,\delta},\;i=1,2
, for
\delta>0
. This property will later allow us to prove the continuity with respect to the measure arguments.

Proposition 7.3
.

Fix
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
t<T
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, in the above setup,

(7.4)

U_{i,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\delta>0,\;\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{i,\delta}_{t,\mu,\nu}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}L\left(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},\alpha_{s}\right)ds\right]\right\}.

Furthermore, for any
R>0
such that
R>\int|x|^{2}\mu(dx)
, there exists a constant
C:=C_{t,\nu,R}
depending on the quantity
\sup_{m\in\mathcal{P}_{2}:\;{\bf d}_{2}^{2}(\delta_{0},m)\leq R}U_{i,ot}^{\varepsilon}(t,m,\nu)
and
T-t
such that for any
\eta>0
with
C\eta<T-t
, there exists an
\eta
-optimal control
\alpha^{\eta}\in\mathcal{A}^{i,C\eta}_{t,\mu,\nu}
.

Proof.

The fact that
U^{\varepsilon}_{i,ot}(t,\mu,\nu)
is less or equal that the right hand side of (
7.4
) is obvious, so it suffices to prove the reverse inequality. Let
\mathbb{N}\ni n\geq 1
,
\delta\in(0,T-t)
and
(\alpha_{s}^{n})_{s\in[t,T]}
be a
\frac{1}{n}
-optimal control for
U^{\varepsilon}_{i,ot}
and
(X^{n}_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}
the associated state process. We denote
\psi(s)=\frac{T-t}{T-t-\delta}(s-t-\delta)+t
and we define the control

\alpha_{s}=\begin{cases}\frac{T-t}{T-t-\delta}\alpha_{\psi(s)}^{n},&\text{ if }s\in[t+\delta,T],\\
0,&\text{ if }s\in[t,t+\delta],\end{cases}

and the process

X_{s}=\begin{cases}X_{\psi(s)}^{n},&\text{ if }s\in[t+\delta,T],\\
X_{t}^{n},&\text{ if }s\in[t,t+\delta].\end{cases}

It straightforward to see that
dX_{s}=\alpha_{s}ds
with
X_{t}=X_{t}^{n}\sim\mu
and
X_{T}=X^{n}_{\psi(T)}=X^{n}_{T}\sim\nu
, therefore
(\alpha_{s})_{s\in[t,T]}\in\mathcal{A}^{i,\delta}_{t,\mu,\nu}
. In the following inequalities the constant
C
might become larger from line to line and remains independent of
n,\delta
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}L\big(\frac{X_{s}}{\varepsilon},
